  \longrightarrow\Omega_V^1
  \longrightarrow A^\vee\longrightarrow0.
  \label{eq:foliation-cotangent}$$ Here the last map is evaluation on $A$. To verify local freeness of the first image, put $N=\langle dc_1,\ldots,dc_d\rangle$ inside $R\otimes_P\Omega_{P/k}^1$. The presentation gives $$\Omega_{R/k}^1\simeq
  \bigl((R\otimes_P\Omega_{P/k}^1)/N\bigr)
  \oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^dR\,dx_i,$$ because the relations have no differential in the $X_i$ directions. Smoothness of $R$ makes the first summand locally free of rank $3-d$. Smoothness of $P$ then makes $N$ a locally free direct summand of rank $d$ in the rank-three bundle $R\otimes_P\Omega_{P/k}^1$. The displayed $d$ generators of $N$ are a basis: locally their surjection from $R^d$ onto a free module of rank $d$ is an isomorphism. This proves the required exactness at every point of the chosen open set.

To identify its transition maps, let $x'_j$ be another such set of functions and write it in the basis $x^I$ over $P$. Taking $p$-th powers and differentiating downstairs shows that, after pullback, the coefficient of $dc_l$ in $d((x'_j)^p)$ is $u_l(x'_j)^p$. On the other hand, the coefficient of the $l$-th basis vector of $A^\vee$ in $dx'_j|_A$ is $u_l(x'_j)$. The first term of Equation <a href="#eq:foliation-cotangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:foliation-cotangent">[eq:foliation-cotangent]</a> is therefore precisely $F_V^*(A^\vee)$. Taking determinants in that sequence gives $$\pi^*\omega_W
  \simeq(\det A)^{-p}\otimes\omega_V\otimes\det A,$$ which is Equation <a href="#eq:foliation-canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:foliation-canonical">[eq:foliation-canonical]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Strong semistability of the tangent bundle

<a id="stmt-prop-strong-semistability"></a>
<div id="prop:strong-semistability" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:c2-nonnegative"></a>
**Proposition 9**. *The tangent bundle $T_V$ is strongly semistable for the slope $c_1(-)HD/\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(-)$. Moreover, $$c_2(V)D\ge0.
  \label{eq:c2-nonnegative}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-strong-semistability"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-strong-semistability</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove semistability by the foliation and rational-curve argument of (C. Xu and Zhang 2019, Lemma 2.10) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[xuzhang2019](#ref-xuzhang2019)], with the quantitative bend-and-break statement specified below. Suppose that $A$ is the first destabilizing term, with $a=\mu(A)>0$ and $d=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}A$. By Lemmas <a href="#lem:destabilizing-foliation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:destabilizing-foliation">7</a> and <a href="#lem:foliation-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:foliation-quotient">8</a>, it has a finite purely inseparable quotient $\pi\colon V\to W$. On the big smooth open set where Equation <a href="#eq:foliation-canonical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:foliation-canonical">[eq:foliation-canonical]</a> holds, put $\Lambda=-\pi^*K_W$. Its extension as a divisor class on $V$ is $$\Lambda=(p-1)c_1(A)-K_V,
  \qquad
  \Lambda HD=(p-1)da>0.$$ No global Cartier property of $K_W$ is needed: its degree will be taken only on curves contained in this smooth open set.

Let $H^{(1)}$ and $D^{(1)}$ denote the corresponding divisor classes on $V^{(1)}$, and set $$H'=\rho^*H^{(1)},\qquad D'=\rho^*D^{(1)}.$$ Then $H'$ is ample and Cartier, $D'$ is nef and Cartier, and $$\pi^*H'=pH,\qquad \pi^*D'=pD.$$ For a positive integer $t$, take sufficiently divisible positive multiples of $H'$ and $H'+tD'$ which are very ample. Their general complete intersection is a smooth projective integral curve $C_t$ wholly contained in the open set under consideration. Here is the avoidance point in this assertion. The complement has dimension at most one. A general first divisor meets it in finitely many points and contains none of its curve components; a general second divisor avoids those finitely many points. Bertini on the smooth open set, and irreducibility for the successive very ample sections, give the required curve.

<a id="eq:bend-break-ratio"></a>
Projection formula, computed where $\pi$ is locally free, cancels both the degree of $\pi$ and the chosen complete-intersection multiples from the degree ratio. It gives $$\begin{aligned}
  \frac{D'C_t}{-K_WC_t}
  &=\frac{pD\,H(H+tD)}{\Lambda H(H+tD)} \notag\\
  &=\frac{pDH^2}{\Lambda H^2+t\Lambda HD}
    \longrightarrow0
    \qquad(t\longrightarrow\infty).
  \label{eq:bend-break-ratio}
\end{aligned}$$ The denominator is positive for all sufficiently large $t$.

We use the following quantitative form of bend-and-break (Jovinelly, Lehmann, and Riedl 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[jlr2026](#ref-jlr2026)]: if $Y$ is projective over an algebraically closed field, $C\subset Y_{\mathrm{sm}}$ is an integral curve with $K_YC<0$, and $N$ is a nef real Cartier divisor, then every closed point of $C$ lies on a rational curve $\Gamma\subset Y$ satisfying $$N\Gamma\le(\dim Y+1)\frac{NC}{-K_YC}.$$ This statement is valid in arbitrary characteristic. Choose $t$ so large that the ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:bend-break-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bend-break-ratio">[eq:bend-break-ratio]</a> is less than $1/4$. Applied on $W$ with $N=D'$, the theorem gives, through every closed point $x\in C_t$, a rational curve $\Gamma$ with $$0\le D'\Gamma<1.$$ As $D'$ is Cartier, this degree is an integer and hence is zero.

We choose $C_t$ to witness the very-general-point contradiction as well. Fix the product of the two chosen very ample linear systems, and let $\mathcal T$ be its nonempty open locus of smooth integral complete intersections avoiding the bad locus above. This parameter space is irreducible. Its universal curve $\mathcal C\to\mathcal T$ is smooth: each fiber is a transverse complete intersection inside $W_{\mathrm{sm}}$, so the relative Jacobian criterion applies. Its evaluation map $\mathcal C\to W$ is dominant. Indeed the ambient incidence of points and pairs of divisors through them is a product of projective bundles over $W$, hence is irreducible and surjects onto $W$; restricting it to the nonempty open parameter locus $\mathcal T$ leaves a dense open subset, whose evaluation still has dense image.

Let $Z_1,Z_2,\ldots$ be proper closed subsets of $V$ outside whose union no $D$-trivial curve passes. The finite map $\pi$ has proper closed images $\pi(Z_j)$. For each $j$, the locus in $\mathcal T$ of curves not contained in $\pi(Z_j)$ is the image of $\mathcal C\setminus\operatorname{ev}^{-1}(\pi(Z_j))$ under the smooth map $\mathcal C\to\mathcal T$. It is open because smooth maps are open, and nonempty because evaluation is dominant. Over the uncountable field, the countably many resulting nonempty opens in $\mathcal T$ have a common $k$-point. On its integral curve $C_t$, each $C_t\cap\pi(Z_j)$ is finite. Choose a closed point $x$ outside their union. Its unique inverse-image point $y\in V$ lies outside every $Z_j$.

The reduced inverse image of the rational curve $\Gamma$ through $x$ contains a curve $\widetilde\Gamma$ through $y$, finite and surjective over $\Gamma$. Projection formula gives $$pD\widetilde\Gamma
  =(\pi^*D')\widetilde\Gamma
  =\deg(\widetilde\Gamma/\Gamma)D'\Gamma=0.$$ This contradicts the choice of $y$. Thus $T_V$ is semistable.

Suppose next that $e\ge1$ is the first integer for which $F_V^{e*}T_V$ is unstable. Let $A'$ be its first Harder–Narasimhan term and $Q'=F_V^{e*}T_V/A'$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:connection-destabilization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:connection-destabilization">6</a> to the semistable sheaf $F_V^{(e-1)*}T_V$ gives a nonzero map $$A'\longrightarrow Q'\otimes\Omega_V^1,
  \qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
  A'\otimes(Q')^\vee\longrightarrow\Omega_V^1$$ on a big open set. The cotangent bundle is semistable of slope zero, because $T_V$ is. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}A'+\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}Q'=3$, at least one tensor factor in the second source has rank one. If $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}Q'=1$, semistability of $A'$ and the rank-one twisting rule give the minimum slope of the source as $\mu(A')-\mu(Q')$. If $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}A'=1$, the quotient $Q'$ need not be semistable; use instead $$\mu_{\min}((Q')^\vee)=-\mu_{\max}(Q')$$ and twist by the rank-one factor $A'$. In both cases, $$\mu_{\min}\bigl(A'\otimes(Q')^\vee\bigr)
  =\mu(A')-\mu_{\max}(Q')>0.$$ This contradicts Equation <a href="#eq:slope-hom-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slope-hom-vanishing">[eq:slope-hom-vanishing]</a>. All Frobenius pullbacks of $T_V$ are therefore semistable.

Finally apply Langer’s Bogomolov inequality (Langer 2004b, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[langer2004](#ref-langer2004)]; see also the addendum (Langer 2004a) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[langeradd2004](#ref-langeradd2004)]. This theorem is formulated for a nef tuple $D_1,\ldots,D_{n-1}$ with numerically nonzero product, and for a strongly semistable torsion-free sheaf gives $\Delta(E)D_2\cdots D_{n-1}\ge0$, where $\Delta(E)=2\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(E)c_2(E)-(\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(E)-1)c_1(E)^2$. Here the tuple is $(H,D)$, whose product is nonzero since $H^2D>0$. For the rank-three tangent bundle this yields $$\bigl(6c_2(V)-2K_V^2\bigr)D\ge0.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:slope-numerical-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slope-numerical-input">[eq:slope-numerical-input]</a> gives $K_V^2D=0$ and proves Equation <a href="#eq:c2-nonnegative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:c2-nonnegative">[eq:c2-nonnegative]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Uniform section bounds

<a id="stmt-lem-degree-zero-line-sections"></a>
<div id="lem:degree-zero-line-sections" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *If a line bundle $L$ has $\deg L\le0$, then $h^0(V,L)\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-degree-zero-line-sections"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-degree-zero-line-sections</summary>

*Proof.* A nonzero section has an effective zero divisor, so its degree is nonnegative and hence must be zero. Suppose that two independent sections $s_0,s_1$ exist. Remove the base locus of the resulting pencil. Through every point $x$ of the remaining open set there is a member of the pencil: take a nonzero linear combination vanishing at $x$. Its divisor has degree zero. Each prime component $S$ through $x$ therefore satisfies $DHS=0$, because all component degrees are nonnegative.

The restriction of $D$ to $S$ is nef and numerically trivial. One way to see the latter assertion, allowing $S$ to be singular, is to take a projective resolution $\sigma\colon\widetilde S\to S$. The class $L_S=\sigma^*(D|_S)$ is nef and is orthogonal to the big and nef class $A_S=\sigma^*(H|_S)$, with $A_S^2>0$. The surface Hodge index theorem (Hartshorne 1977, vol. 52, chap. V, Section 1) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[hartshorne1977](#ref-hartshorne1977)] makes the intersection form negative definite on $A_S^\perp$, whereas nefness gives $L_S^2\ge0$. Hence $L_S$ is numerically zero, and the projection formula gives the assertion on $S$. A projective integral curve in $S$ through $x$, obtained for example as a component of a sufficiently ample section through $x$, consequently has $D$-degree zero. Choosing $x$ very general contradicts the prepared-cycle property. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-semistable-hom-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:semistable-hom-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hom-rank-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (A uniform Hom bound). *If $G_1,G_2$ are torsion-free semistable sheaves of slope zero, then $$\dim_k\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(G_1,G_2)\le\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(G_1)\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}(G_2).
  \label{eq:hom-rank-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-semistable-hom-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-semistable-hom-bound</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose both sheaves are stable. For a nonzero map, its image has slope at least zero as a quotient of $G_1$, and at most zero as a subsheaf of $G_2$. A positive-rank kernel would make the first inequality strict by stability of $G_1$. An image of rank smaller than $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}G_2$ would make the second inequality strict by stability of $G_2$. Thus every nonzero map has full rank, and the two sheaves have the same rank, say $n$.

The determinant of each map is a global section of $$L=\det(G_2)\otimes\det(G_1)^{-1},\qquad\deg L=0.$$ It is initially defined on the locally free open set and extends across its codimension-at-least-two complement. By Lemma <a href="#lem:degree-zero-line-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degree-zero-line-sections">10</a>, $H^0(V,L)$ has dimension at most one. If $f,g$ were linearly independent maps, fix a nonzero section $\tau$ spanning this space and write $$\det(sf+tg)=P(s,t)\tau$$ for a homogeneous polynomial $P$ of degree $n$. Since $f$ has full rank, $P$ is nonzero; since $n>0$ and $k$ is algebraically closed, $P$ vanishes at some point of $\mathbb P^1(k)$. The corresponding linear combination is a nonzero map by independence, but has zero determinant, a contradiction. Thus the Hom space between stable factors has dimension at most one.

Every slope-zero semistable sheaf admits a finite filtration by saturated subsheaves with torsion-free stable factors of slope zero. Indeed if it is not stable, a smaller-rank equal-slope subsheaf can be saturated; its degree cannot increase strictly because of semistability. Both it and the quotient are semistable of slope zero, and induction on rank finishes the construction. Apply the left-exact Hom sequences successively to such filtrations of $G_1$ and $G_2$. The dimension of the Hom space is at most the sum of the dimensions between all pairs of stable factors. Each summand is at most one, and the numbers of factors are at most the respective ranks. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:hom-rank-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hom-rank-bound">[eq:hom-rank-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-polar-bounds"></a>
<div id="prop:polar-bounds" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:uniform-bundle-sections"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Polar-part bounds). *Let $G$ be a semistable vector bundle of slope zero. For every integer $i$, $$h^0\bigl(V,G(iD)\bigr)\le\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}G,
  \qquad
  h^0\bigl(V,G^{\otimes2}(iD)\bigr)\le(\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}G)^2.
  \label{eq:uniform-bundle-sections}$$ For each fixed bundle $J=G$ or $J=G^{\otimes2}$, the increasing spaces <a id="eq:polar-part-system"></a>$$H^0\bigl(V,J(iD)/J\bigr),\qquad i=1,2,\ldots,
  \label{eq:polar-part-system}$$ have bounded dimension and eventually stabilize under their natural injections. In particular their elements have a uniform bound on their actual pole orders along every prime component of $D$. This pole bound may depend on $J$; it is uniform in the cut $i$ and in the chosen section.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-polar-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-polar-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* All the twists $G(iD)$ are semistable of slope zero. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:semistable-hom-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:semistable-hom-bound">11</a> first to $\mathcal O_V,G(iD)$ and then to $G^\vee,G(iD)$. The identities $$H^0\bigl(V,G(iD)\bigr)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\bigl(\mathcal O_V,G(iD)\bigr),
  \qquad
  H^0\bigl(V,G^{\otimes2}(iD)\bigr)
    =\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\bigl(G^\vee,G(iD)\bigr)$$ give Equation <a href="#eq:uniform-bundle-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-bundle-sections">[eq:uniform-bundle-sections]</a>. In particular the second bound uses the two semistable bundles $G^\vee$ and $G(iD)$, without needing semistability of $G^{\otimes2}$.

For $i>0$, the cohomology sequence of $0\to J\to J(iD)\to J(iD)/J\to0$ gives $$h^0\bigl(V,J(iD)/J\bigr)
  \le h^0\bigl(V,J(iD)\bigr)+h^1(V,J).$$ The right side is bounded independently of $i$. Effectivity of $D$ gives injections of the quotient sheaves as $i$ increases, hence injections of their finite-dimensional spaces of global sections. A bounded increasing sequence of integer dimensions is eventually constant, so these spaces stabilize.

More explicitly, choose $i_0$ after stabilization. Every global polar part, at every later cut, is then the image of an element of $H^0(V,J(i_0D)/J)$. At the generic point of a prime component $D_\alpha$ of multiplicity $m_\alpha$, a local bundle frame measures its pole order coefficientwise. Such an image has pole order at most $i_0m_\alpha$ there. This bound is independent of the later cut and of the element chosen. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-no-scalar-poles"></a>
<div id="cor:no-scalar-poles" class="corollary">

**Corollary 13** (No scalar polar parts). *For every $i>0$, $$H^0\bigl(V,\mathcal O_V(iD)/\mathcal O_V\bigr)=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-no-scalar-poles"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-no-scalar-poles</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:polar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polar-bounds">12</a> to $G=\mathcal O_V$. Suppose a nonzero scalar polar part $\eta$ exists. It has a genuine negative valuation at the generic point of some component of $D$. To justify this detection, take local rational-function representatives. If all their component valuations were nonnegative, they would be regular by normality of $V$; they already have no poles off $D$. The quotient section would then be zero.

For every $e\ge0$, raising the local representatives to their $p^e$-th powers defines a global section $$\eta^{p^e}\in
  H^0\bigl(V,\mathcal O_V(p^eiD)/\mathcal O_V\bigr).$$ This operation is well defined on polar parts: two representatives differing by a regular function have $p^e$-th powers differing by a regular function. At the selected component the negative valuation is multiplied by $p^e$. These pole orders are unbounded, contradicting the uniform bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:polar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polar-bounds">12</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:jets"></a>
### Jets and the three rows

<a id="eq:canonical-p-power"></a>
We retain the prepared data of Proposition <a href="#prop:prepared-cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prepared-cycle">2</a>. Thus $V$ is a smooth projective threefold, and $$D=\sum_\alpha m_\alpha D_\alpha$$ is an effective Cartier divisor with connected support, $\gcd_\alpha m_\alpha=1$, and $D|_{D_\alpha}$ numerically trivial for every $\alpha$. In particular, $D^2A=0$ for every divisor $A$. The line bundle $\mathcal O_D(D)$ is torsion. Near $D$ there is an isomorphism $$\label{eq:canonical-p-power}
 \omega_V^{\otimes p^b}\simeq\mathcal O_V(B_*),\qquad
 B_*=\sum_\alpha b_\alpha D_\alpha,
 \quad b_\alpha\in\mathbb Z,$$ and $K_V^2D=0$. We shall use $c_2(V)D\geq0$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:strong-semistability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:strong-semistability">9</a> and the absence of scalar polar parts from Corollary <a href="#cor:no-scalar-poles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-scalar-poles">13</a>.

The argument in this section concerns finite jets and their ranks. Its two outputs are a trivialization of the canonical line on the completion up to an integral divisor supported on $D$, and the equality $c_2(V)D=0$ together with the limit $p/(p+1)$ for the normalized last trivialization widths. The width at period $rp^e$ is the largest integer $s$ for which $\mathcal O(rp^eD)$ is trivial on $sD$. Its only passage to a formal limit will be an explicit inverse-limit argument for finite-dimensional spaces of sections.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Finite pages and duality

Write $\widehat{\mathcal O}_V$ for the completion along $D$, regarded as a sheaf on $|D|$, and set $$\mathcal R=\widehat{\mathcal O}_V(*D).$$ Choose a finite cover by the restrictions to $|D|$ of affine open subsets of $V$, refining it so that $D$ is principal and the bundles under consideration are free on each member. All intersections of these affine opens are affine, since $V$ is separated. For a vector bundle $E$, let $C_i^\bullet(E)$ be the resulting Čech complex with coefficients in the completion of $E(iD)$. These complexes give an increasing filtration, indexed by $i\in\mathbb Z$, on the Laurent Čech complex.

<a id="eq:jet-quotient"></a>
Here are the elementary completeness facts we need. On an affine chart with local equation $f$ for $D$, adic completion is flat and $f$ remains a non-zero-divisor. Thus the completed lattices embed in their localization. A section of the localization on a quasi-compact open lies in one lattice: finitely many local pole bounds have a common upper bound, and the resulting lattice sections glue. Consequently every Laurent cochain has a finite upper weight. Each $C_i^\bullet(E)$ is complete and separated for the filtration by $C_{i-s}^\bullet(E)$, $s\geq1$. Exactness of completion for finite modules gives $$\label{eq:jet-quotient}
 \begin{gathered}
 C_i^\bullet(E)/C_{i-s}^\bullet(E)
 \quad\text{computes}\quad H^\bullet\bigl(V,P_i^s(E)\bigr),\\
 P_i^s(E)=\frac{E(iD)}{E((i-s)D)}.
\end{gathered}$$ Indeed its terms are exactly the sections of this algebraic quotient on the affine intersections. Thus $P_i^s(E)$ retains the $s$ layers at weights $i-s+1,\ldots,i$; we also call $i$ the upper cut. We abbreviate $E^!=E^\vee\otimes\omega_V$.

For $s\geq1$, consider a leading cochain, that is, a class in $C_i^a(E)/C_{i-1}^a(E)$, where $a$ is its cohomological degree. The cycle condition below asks whether this class has a representative closed modulo $C_{i-s}^\bullet(E)$. The boundary condition asks whether it is the leading term of a coboundary with a primitive in $C_{i+s-1}^{a-1}(E)$. Thus the index $s$ records both the precision of closedness and the allowed upper cut of a primitive. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 Z_s^{i,a}&=
 \{[x]:x\in C_i^a(E),\ \partial x\in C_{i-s}^{a+1}(E)\},\\
 U_s^{i,a}&=
 \{[\partial y]:y\in C_{i+s-1}^{a-1}(E),\
                         \partial y\in C_i^a(E)\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Every exact cochain is closed, so $U_s^{i,a}\subseteq Z_s^{i,a}$. Put $$E_s^{i,a}(E)=Z_s^{i,a}/U_s^{i,a},\qquad
 g_s^a(i;E)=\dim_k E_s^{i,a}(E).$$ The notation $E_s^{i,a}(E)$ denotes a page space, not a new bundle.

<a id="stmt-lem-page-calculus"></a>
<div id="lem:page-calculus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:bottom-endpoint"></a>
<a id="eq:top-endpoint"></a>
<a id="eq:middle-recurrence"></a>
<a id="eq:periodic-euler"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Finite-page calculus). *The operation $x\mapsto\partial x$ induces maps $$d_s:E_s^{i,a}(E)\longrightarrow E_s^{i-s,a+1}(E)$$ whose cohomology is the next page. At page one, $E_1^{i,a}(E)=H^a(V,P_i^1(E))$; every page has only rows $a=0,1,2$. In particular, with $\delta_s^a(i)=g_s^a(i;E)-g_{s+1}^a(i;E)$ for $a=0,2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 g_s^0(i;E)
 &=\dim\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(H^0(P_i^s(E))\longrightarrow H^0(P_i^1(E))\bigr),
 \label{eq:bottom-endpoint}\\
 g_s^2(i;E)&=g_s^0(1-i;E^!),\label{eq:top-endpoint}\\
 g_{s+1}^1(i;E)
 &=g_s^1(i;E)-\delta_s^0(i+s)-\delta_s^2(i-s).
 \label{eq:middle-recurrence}
\end{aligned}$$ If $M>0$ and $\mathcal O(MD)|_{sD}$ is trivial, the page-$s$ ranks are periodic in $i$ modulo $M$, and $$\label{eq:periodic-euler}
 \sum_{i\bmod M}\bigl(g_s^0(i;E)-g_s^1(i;E)+g_s^2(i;E)\bigr)
 =\sum_{i\bmod M}\chi(P_i^1(E)).$$ Finally, at each fixed weight $i$, the stable image in Equation <a href="#eq:bottom-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:bottom-endpoint">[eq:bottom-endpoint]</a> is precisely the image of the global sections of the completed bundle $\widehat{E(iD)}$ in $H^0(P_i^1(E))$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-page-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-page-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* If two representatives of a leading cochain differ by $y\in C_{i-1}^a$, and both have coboundary in $C_{i-s}$, their coboundaries differ by a target boundary coming from $C_{i-1}=C_{(i-s)+s-1}$. Adding an exact representative has no effect either. This proves that $d_s$ is well defined, and $d_s^2=0$ follows from $\partial^2=0$.

If $d_s[x]=0$, the leading term of $\partial x$ is represented by $\partial y$ with $y\in C_{i-1}^a$ and $\partial y\in C_{i-s}^{a+1}$. Replacing $x$ by $x-y$ makes its coboundary lie in $C_{i-s-1}^{a+1}$. Thus the kernel is represented by $Z_{s+1}^{i,a}$. The incoming image enlarges $U_s^{i,a}$ to $U_{s+1}^{i,a}$: its representatives are exactly the leading terms of $\partial z\in C_i^a$ with $z\in C_{i+s}^{a-1}$. This proves the next-page assertion. Equation <a href="#eq:jet-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:jet-quotient">[eq:jet-quotient]</a> identifies page one. Since the quotients are supported on the projective surface scheme $D$, their cohomology is zero outside degrees $0,1,2$; the same is true of every subsequent page.

The differentials through a middle-row term are arranged as $$\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large]
 E_s^{i+s,0}(E) \arrow[r,"d_s"] &
 E_s^{i,1}(E) \arrow[r,"d_s"] & E_s^{i-s,2}(E).
 \end{tikzcd}$$ Moving right lowers weight by $s$ and raises cohomological degree by one; the composite is zero. There is no incoming differential in degree zero. Its representatives are exactly the sections of $P_i^s(E)$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:bottom-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:bottom-endpoint">[eq:bottom-endpoint]</a>.

For the other endpoint, consider the inclusion of the bottom layer $$H^2(P_i^1(E))\longrightarrow H^2(P_{i+s-1}^s(E)).$$ There is no outgoing differential in degree two. More explicitly, the kernel calculation above and vanishing of the degree-three page imply successively that $Z_s^{i,2}=Z_1^{i,2}$. A class represented by $x\in C_i^2$, with $\partial x\in C_{i-1}^3$, dies under this inclusion exactly when $x-\partial y\in C_{i-1}^2$ for some $y\in C_{i+s-1}^1$. Its kernel is therefore $U_s^{i,2}/U_1^{i,2}$, and the image has dimension $g_s^2(i;E)$.

The two-term locally free presentation of $P_j^s(E)$ gives $$\mathcal E xt^1(P_j^s(E),\omega_V)
 =\frac{E^!((s-j)D)}{E^!(-jD)}=P_{s-j}^s(E^!),$$ and all other sheaf Ext groups vanish. Serre duality on the smooth projective threefold consequently identifies $H^2(P_j^s(E))^\vee$ with $H^0(P_{s-j}^s(E^!))$ (Hartshorne 1977, vol. 52, chap. III, Section 7) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[hartshorne1977](#ref-hartshorne1977)]. Under this identification the dual of the displayed inclusion is $$H^0(P_{1-i}^s(E^!))\longrightarrow H^0(P_{1-i}^1(E^!)).$$ One can check the map, as well as its index, directly: the map of the Cartier presentations is the identity on $E((i-1)D)$ and the inclusion on their upper terms, so dualizing gives precisely the indicated projection. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:top-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:top-endpoint">[eq:top-endpoint]</a>. The ranks of the two differentials adjacent to the middle term are $\delta_s^0(i+s)$ and $\delta_s^2(i-s)$, respectively. Taking its cohomology proves Equation <a href="#eq:middle-recurrence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:middle-recurrence">[eq:middle-recurrence]</a>.

Triviality of $\mathcal O(MD)$ on $sD$ identifies each quotient of width $t\leq s$ with its translate by $M$, compatibly with its layer maps. Equations <a href="#eq:bottom-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:bottom-endpoint">[eq:bottom-endpoint]</a> and <a href="#eq:top-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:top-endpoint">[eq:top-endpoint]</a> therefore give periodicity of both outer rows at every such width. Page one is periodic in all degrees; Equation <a href="#eq:middle-recurrence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:middle-recurrence">[eq:middle-recurrence]</a> then gives periodicity of the middle row by induction. If $e_t(i)=g_t^0(i)-g_t^1(i)+g_t^2(i)$, that equation yields $$e_{t+1}(i)-e_t(i)=
 -\delta_t^0(i)-\delta_t^2(i)
 +\delta_t^0(i+t)+\delta_t^2(i-t).$$ For $t<s$ all four functions are periodic modulo $M$. Their sums cancel, proving Equation <a href="#eq:periodic-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:periodic-euler">[eq:periodic-euler]</a> by induction from page one.

For the last assertion, set $A_s=H^0(P_i^s(E))$ and $$B_s=\bigcap_{t\geq s}\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(A_t\longrightarrow A_s).$$ Each $A_s$ is finite dimensional, so the intersection defining $B_s$ is attained at a finite stage. Choosing one stage beyond stabilization at both $s$ and $s+1$ shows that $B_{s+1}\longrightarrow B_s$ is surjective. Every element of $B_1$ therefore admits compatible lifts in all the $B_s$. Their inverse limit is a global section of $\widehat{E(iD)}$, since sections commute with inverse limits of sheaves. Conversely a completed section restricts to the image of every $A_s$. This proves the assertion directly. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Scalar jets and their last widths

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-jets"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-jets" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:scalar-endpoint"></a>
**Lemma 15** (The scalar jet rule). *Let $u<i$ be integers and let $\xi\in H^0(\mathcal O(iD)/\mathcal O(uD))$ be nonzero. There is an integer $t$ with $u<t\leq i$ such that local lifts of $\xi$ have pole order exactly $tm_\alpha$ at every $D_\alpha$. Those lifts give a trivializing section of $\mathcal O(tD)$ on $(t-u)D$. In particular, $$\label{eq:scalar-endpoint}
 g_s^0(i;\mathcal O_V)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&\mathcal O(iD)|_{sD}\simeq\mathcal O_{sD},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-jets</summary>

*Proof.* Choose local algebraic lifts $f_\lambda$ of $\xi$ on a finite affine cover. Membership in $\mathcal O(uD)$ is checked by valuations at the prime divisors of the smooth, hence normal, variety $V$. Thus some component detects the nonzero quotient section. The maximum of the ratios $-\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{D_\alpha}(f_\lambda)/m_\alpha$ that exceed $u$ is a well-defined rational number $t\in(u,i]$: changing a lift adds a section of $\mathcal O(uD)$ and cannot alter such a valuation. All lifts belong to $\mathcal O(tD)$ in the sense of these rational divisor bounds.

Choose a positive integer $n$ with $nt\in\mathbb Z$. Then $f_\lambda^n\in\mathcal O(ntD)$, and these sections agree on $D_{\mathrm{red}}$. Indeed $f_\lambda-f_\mu\in\mathcal O(uD)$, and in the factorization of $f_\lambda^n-f_\mu^n$ each term has one factor of pole ratio at most $u$ and $n-1$ factors of pole ratio at most $t$. After normalization in $\mathcal O(ntD)$ its valuation at every $D_\alpha$ is at least $(t-u)m_\alpha>0$. They therefore define a section of $\mathcal O(ntD)|_{D_{\mathrm{red}}}$, nonzero on a component where the maximum is attained.

The restriction of this line bundle to every integral component is numerically trivial. A nonzero section of a numerically trivial line bundle on a projective integral surface has no zeros: a nonempty effective Cartier zero divisor has positive intersection with an ample divisor. It is consequently nowhere zero on that component. At an intersection with another component its common value is nonzero, so its restriction to that component is also nonzero and nowhere zero. Connectedness of $|D|$ propagates this conclusion to all components.

It follows that $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{D_\alpha}(f_\lambda)=-tm_\alpha$ for every $\alpha$. In particular every $tm_\alpha$ is an integer. Primitivity of the cycle, or an integral linear combination of the $m_\alpha$ equal to one, gives $t\in\mathbb Z$. The original lifts are now sections of the honest line bundle $\mathcal O(tD)$, are units in it along $D$, and agree modulo $\mathcal O(uD)$. Since $$\frac{\mathcal O(tD)}{\mathcal O(uD)}
 =\mathcal O(tD)|_{(t-u)D},$$ they give the asserted trivialization.

A jet of width $s$ at upper weight $i$ has nonzero image in the leading layer exactly when the uniform ratio just proved is $t=i$. This proves existence in Equation <a href="#eq:scalar-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scalar-endpoint">[eq:scalar-endpoint]</a>, in both directions. To see that the image dimension is one, take two such jets. Their normalized unit sections on $D_{\mathrm{red}}$ are proportional: a connected reduced projective scheme over $k$ has only constant global functions. Subtract the appropriate multiple. If its leading image were still nonzero, the preceding argument would again give a unit on the reduction with ratio $i$, contradicting the cancellation. Thus the two leading images are proportional. ◻

</details>

The organizing viewpoint of tracking torsion and triviality on infinitesimal thickenings was informed by Totaro’s treatment of these obstructions (Totaro 2007, Lemma 4.1 and Section 6) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[totaro2007](#ref-totaro2007)]. The elementary width properties are proved below; the later three-row rank calculation and Cartier descent are not inputs from that work.

<a id="eq:width-bounds"></a>
Let $r$ be the order of $\mathcal O_D(D)$. For $e\geq0$ put <a id="eq:width-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:width-definition}
 M_e=rp^e,\qquad
 d_e=\max\{s\geq1:\mathcal O(M_eD)|_{sD}\simeq\mathcal O_{sD}\}.$$ These maxima exist and satisfy $$\label{eq:width-bounds}
 1\leq d_e<M_e,\qquad d_{e+1}\geq p d_e,
 \qquad \frac{d_e}{M_e}\nearrow a_*\in(0,1].$$ In fact a trivialization on $M_eD$ would give, by Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-jets">15</a>, a nonzero section of $\mathcal O(M_eD)/\mathcal O$, contrary to Corollary <a href="#cor:no-scalar-poles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-scalar-poles">13</a>. Triviality at width one follows from the definition of $r$. Taking $p$th powers of local trivializing sections multiplies the gluing precision by $p$ and gives $d_{e+1}\geq pd_e$. Division by $M_{e+1}=pM_e$ proves monotonicity and the positive limit. If $r=1$, already $1\leq d_0<M_0=1$ is a contradiction; we may therefore continue with $r>1$.

<a id="eq:scalar-last-width"></a>
We will repeatedly use the following precise consequence of nilpotence. If a line bundle $L$ on a finite thickening $T$ of $D$ is trivial on $D$, then its class has $p$-power order. Indeed its transition functions can be chosen in $1+J$, where $J$ is the nilpotent ideal of $D$ in $T$. For $p^N$ at least a nilpotence exponent, $(1+x)^{p^N}=1$ for $x\in J$. Thus $L^{\otimes p^N}$ is trivial. Multiplication by an integer prime to $p$ is injective on such classes, by Bézout’s identity, as well as surjective on their cyclic subgroups. It follows that $$\label{eq:scalar-last-width}
 \text{the last width at }i=jM_e\text{ is }d_e
 \quad\text{if }p\nmid j.$$ This includes negative $j$. If $r\nmid i$, no scalar leading jet occurs even at width one. Every nonzero multiple of $r$ has a unique expression $jM_e$ with $p\nmid j$; at $i=0$ the scalar entry persists at every width. These observations classify all scalar outer-row entries and drops.

<a id="eq:scalar-euler"></a>
Finally Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch on the smooth threefold gives $$\label{eq:scalar-euler}
 \chi(P_i^1(\mathcal O_V))
 =\chi(\mathcal O(iD))-\chi(\mathcal O((i-1)D))
 =\frac{Dc_2(V)}{12}=:\gamma\geq0.$$ Indeed the terms involving $D^3$, $D^2K_V$, and $DK_V^2$ vanish, leaving the displayed linear term in the threefold Riemann–Roch polynomial (Fulton 1998, vol. 2, sec. 15) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[fulton1998](#ref-fulton1998)].

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Canonical jets and a formal canonical root

For $E=\mathcal O_V$, Equation <a href="#eq:top-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:top-endpoint">[eq:top-endpoint]</a> computes the top-row rank at weight $i$ from leading jets of $\omega_V$ at weight $1-i$. We need to describe that row as explicitly as the scalar bottom row. We first show that canonical jets survive to arbitrarily large widths. Their $p^b$th powers then let the scalar rule identify $\widehat{\omega}_V$ with the line of an integral divisor supported on $D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-forced-canonical-jets"></a>
<div id="lem:forced-canonical-jets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *There are nonzero leading jets of $\omega_V$ of arbitrarily large width, allowing their upper weight to vary.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-forced-canonical-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-forced-canonical-jets</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose instead that $g_s^0(l;\omega_V)=0$ for every $l$ and every $s\geq S$, for some $S$. By Equation <a href="#eq:top-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:top-endpoint">[eq:top-endpoint]</a>, the top row for $E=\mathcal O_V$ then vanishes at all these widths. Take $e$ sufficiently large that $s_e=d_{e-1}+1\geq S$. At page $s_e$ the bottom row is one precisely at multiples of $M_e$, by Equation <a href="#eq:scalar-last-width" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scalar-last-width">[eq:scalar-last-width]</a> and $s_e\leq d_e$. This page is periodic modulo $M_e$. Equations <a href="#eq:periodic-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:periodic-euler">[eq:periodic-euler]</a> and <a href="#eq:scalar-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scalar-euler">[eq:scalar-euler]</a> give $$\sum_{i\bmod M_e}g_{s_e}^1(i;\mathcal O_V)
 =1-M_e\gamma\leq1.$$ At differential length $d_e$, the bottom entries at $jM_e$, $p\nmid j$, each drop by one. By Equation <a href="#eq:middle-recurrence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:middle-recurrence">[eq:middle-recurrence]</a> these drops consume one middle-row dimension at $-d_e+jM_e$. Middle-row dimensions can only decrease as the page increases. Periodicity and the preceding upper bound therefore force the initial middle row to consist exactly of one entry at $-d_e\pmod{M_e}$.

Between pages $s_e$ and $s_{e+1}=d_e+1$ these are the only drops: there is no top row, and all scalar entries with a different last-width index either disappeared earlier or have last width at least $d_{e+1}$. Modulo $M_{e+1}=pM_e$, the drops remove all the $p$ lifts of the middle entry except its unshifted lift. Thus at page $s_{e+1}$ the middle row consists of one entry at $-d_e\pmod{M_{e+1}}$. Applying the same argument with $e+1$ says that it must instead lie at $-d_{e+1}\pmod{M_{e+1}}$. But $0<d_e<d_{e+1}<M_{e+1}$, so these residues are different. This contradiction proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-canonical-formal-root"></a>
<div id="prop:canonical-formal-root" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:canonical-formal-root"></a>
**Proposition 17** (The canonical formal root). *Every coefficient of $B_*$ in Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-p-power" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-p-power">[eq:canonical-p-power]</a> is divisible by $p^b$. Writing $B_*=p^bB_0$ with $B_0$ integral, there is an isomorphism on the completion along $D$, $$\label{eq:canonical-formal-root}
 \widehat{\omega}_V\simeq\widehat{\mathcal O}_V(B_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-canonical-formal-root"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-canonical-formal-root</summary>

*Proof.* Put $P=p^b$, and choose a sequence of nonzero leading canonical jets of widths $s\to\infty$, with upper weights $l$ that may vary. Raising local representatives to the $P$th power and using Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-p-power" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-p-power">[eq:canonical-p-power]</a> gives a scalar jet with bounds $$A=PlD+B_*,\qquad B=P(l-s)D+B_*.$$ The lower bound is multiplied by $P$ exactly because $P$ is a power of the characteristic. Set $\beta_\alpha=b_\alpha/m_\alpha$ and choose an integer $c_*\geq\max_\alpha\beta_\alpha$. Enlarging both bounds gives a section of $$\frac{\mathcal O((Pl+c_*)D)}{\mathcal O((P(l-s)+c_*)D)}.$$ This section is nonzero for all sufficiently large $s$, uniformly in $l$. To check this, at a component detecting the original leading layer, a local coefficient $f$ in an ordinary frame of $\omega_V$ has $-\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{D_\alpha}(f)/m_\alpha>l-1$. Its powered scalar coefficient has pole ratio $$-P\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{D_\alpha}(f)}{m_\alpha}
       +\beta_\alpha>P(l-1)+\min_\alpha\beta_\alpha.$$ The new lower bound is $P(l-s)+c_*$, which is smaller for large $s$.

<a id="eq:canonical-ratio-bound"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-jets">15</a> now shows that all these powered coefficients have one integral pole ratio $t$. The preceding lower estimate and the upper bound give a constant $C$, independent of $s,l$, such that $$\label{eq:canonical-ratio-bound}
 \begin{gathered}
 |t-Pl|\leq C,\\
 \mathcal O(tD)\text{ is trivial to a width at least }Ps-C.
 \end{gathered}$$ For each fixed $J$, widths greater than $d_{J-1}$ can occur only at multiples of $M_J$, by the scalar classification; zero is allowed as a multiple. Since the widths in Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-ratio-bound">[eq:canonical-ratio-bound]</a> tend to infinity, we can assign integers $J\to\infty$ along this sequence such that $M_J\mid t$.

<a id="eq:canonical-order-identity"></a>
Fix a component $D_\alpha$. If $f$ is the original local coefficient in a frame of $\omega_V$, the local generator of $\mathcal O(B_*)$ has order $-b_\alpha$. Consequently $$\label{eq:canonical-order-identity}
 b_\alpha-tm_\alpha=P\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{D_\alpha}(f).$$ For large $J$, the integer $t$ is divisible by $P$. Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-order-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-order-identity">[eq:canonical-order-identity]</a> therefore shows that $P\mid b_\alpha$ for every $\alpha$. This proves $B_*=PB_0$ with $B_0$ integral.

For the same sequence put $h_0=t/P$. The order identity says that the original local representatives are sections of $$L_{h_0}=\omega_V(-B_0+h_0D)$$ near $D$: their coefficient orders are exactly those required by this line bundle. They have no additional poles near $D$, since the original representatives were sections of $\omega_V(lD)$ and membership in these divisor lattices is checked on the normal variety in codimension one. Moreover $L_{h_0}^{\otimes P}\simeq\mathcal O(tD)$, and the $P$th powers of these sections are units along all of $D$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-jets">15</a>. The original sections are therefore themselves units along $D$. They agree modulo the original lower lattice $\omega_V((l-s)D)$. Hence they trivialize $L_{h_0}$ on the effective thickness $$T=(h_0-l+s)D-B_0.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-ratio-bound">[eq:canonical-ratio-bound]</a>, $|h_0-l|$ is bounded independently of the sequence. Thus $T$ contains $nD$ for integers $n\to\infty$. Also $M_J\mid t$ implies $M_{J-b}\mid h_0$ once $J\geq b$. The line bundle $\mathcal O(h_0D)$ is therefore trivial on $d_{J-b}D$, and $d_{J-b}\to\infty$ by Equation <a href="#eq:width-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:width-bounds">[eq:width-bounds]</a>. Removing this twist shows that $L=\omega_V(-B_0)$ is trivial on arbitrarily large finite thickenings $nD$.

We finally choose these trivializations compatibly. Apply the stabilized-image argument in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:page-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:page-calculus">14</a> to the spaces $H^0(nD,L|_{nD})$, also including restriction to $D_{\mathrm{red}}$. The latter line bundle is trivial, and its section space is one dimensional because $D_{\mathrm{red}}$ is connected, reduced, and projective. The image from every sufficiently large thickening contains a unit, hence is that full one-dimensional space. The surjective maps between stabilized images therefore lift a chosen nonzero section on $D_{\mathrm{red}}$ compatibly to all thickenings. Each lift is a unit, as may be checked after reduction, and their inverse limit trivializes $\widehat L$. This gives Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-formal-root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-formal-root">[eq:canonical-formal-root]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The two endpoint residues and the limiting width

Write $B_0=\sum_\alpha b_{0,\alpha}D_\alpha$, and now use $h$ for the integer offset <a id="eq:canonical-offset"></a>$$\label{eq:canonical-offset}
 h=-\left\lfloor\min_\alpha
                   \frac{b_{0,\alpha}}{m_\alpha}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad q=1-h.$$ The formal isomorphism of Proposition <a href="#prop:canonical-formal-root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:canonical-formal-root">17</a> identifies the finite canonical jet lattices with scalar lattices having upper and lower bounds $lD+B_0$ and $(l-s)D+B_0$. All these finite quotients are algebraic sheaves supported on finite thickenings of $D$. Indeed the localized $\widehat{\mathcal O}_V$-linear isomorphism respects every nested divisor lattice. Each width-$s$ quotient is killed by $\mathcal I_D^s$, where $\mathcal I_D=\mathcal O_V(-D)$; since $\widehat{\mathcal O}_V/\mathcal I_D^s\widehat{\mathcal O}_V=\mathcal O_{sD}$, the induced map is an algebraic $\mathcal O_{sD}$-linear map, compatible with the leading-layer projections.

<a id="eq:canonical-scalar-ratio"></a>
We first give the exact canonical counterpart of the scalar rule. For all sufficiently large $s$, a canonical jet at weight $l$ with nonzero leading image has uniform integral scalar pole ratio $$\label{eq:canonical-scalar-ratio}
 t=l-h.$$ To prove this, enlarge the two bounds to uniform multiples of $D$ as in the preceding proof. A component detecting the leading image has ratio within a fixed distance of $l$, so for sufficiently large $s$ the enlarged quotient still detects it. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-jets">15</a> gives a uniform integral ratio $t$. Set $\beta_0=\min_\alpha b_{0,\alpha}/m_\alpha$. Fitting under every upper bound, but not under the preceding layer, is exactly the pair of inequalities $$l-1+\beta_0<t\leq l+\beta_0.$$ Its unique integral solution is $t=l+\lfloor\beta_0\rfloor=l-h$.

<a id="eq:canonical-endpoint-rule"></a>
The normalized unit sections of $\mathcal O(tD)$ glue on the irregular thickness <a id="eq:irregular-thickness"></a>$$\label{eq:irregular-thickness}
 T_s=(s-h)D-B_0,$$ because this is the difference between $tD$ and the lower bound $(l-s)D+B_0$. Conversely a trivialization of $\mathcal O(tD)$ on $T_s$, with $t=l-h$, includes in the canonical quotient and has nonzero leading image. Indeed $tD\leq
lD+B_0$, whereas the inequalities above prevent inclusion in the preceding upper lattice. The leading image has dimension one: normalized unit sections are proportional on $D_{\mathrm{red}}$, and their difference cannot retain the same leading ratio after that reduction cancels, by the scalar rule. We have proved $$\label{eq:canonical-endpoint-rule}
 g_s^0(l;\omega_V)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&\mathcal O((l-h)D)|_{T_s}\simeq\mathcal O_{T_s},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad(s\text{ sufficiently large}).$$

<a id="eq:thickness-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:canonical-width-comparison"></a>
The comparison with ordinary widths is uniform and quite explicit. Put $$C_0=h+\left\lceil\max_\alpha
                    \frac{b_{0,\alpha}}{m_\alpha}\right\rceil
 \geq0.$$ Then, coefficient by coefficient, $$\label{eq:thickness-comparison}
 (s-C_0)D\leq T_s\leq sD.$$ For $e$ sufficiently large, let $d'_e$ be the last width $s$ for which $\mathcal O(M_eD)|_{T_s}$ is trivial. The thicknesses are nested as $s$ increases. Equation <a href="#eq:thickness-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:thickness-comparison">[eq:thickness-comparison]</a> and the definition of $d_e$ give $$\label{eq:canonical-width-comparison}
 d_e\leq d'_e\leq d_e+C_0.$$ In particular this last width is finite and exists in the range where Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-endpoint-rule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-endpoint-rule">[eq:canonical-endpoint-rule]</a> applies. Once $T_s$ contains $D$, the nilpotent-kernel argument used above shows that the last width is the same for $t=jM_e$ whenever $p\nmid j$. Also such a trivialization requires $r\mid t$; for $t\ne0$ the unique expression $t=jM_e$, $p\nmid j$, classifies its last width. The value $t=0$ persists forever. To check that the large-$e$ rules exhaust the entries at large width, observe from Equation <a href="#eq:thickness-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:thickness-comparison">[eq:thickness-comparison]</a> that an entry at width $s$ gives an ordinary scalar trivialization to width $s-C_0$. For each fixed $J$, taking $s-C_0>d_{J-1}$ forces $M_J\mid t$. Thus every nonzero index occurring at sufficiently large width belongs to the stated range of last-width indices.

<a id="stmt-prop-scalar-widths"></a>
<div id="prop:scalar-widths" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:limiting-width"></a>
**Proposition 18** (The limiting scalar widths). *The scalar widths satisfy $$\label{eq:limiting-width}
 a_*=\lim_{e\to\infty}\frac{d_e}{M_e}
 =\frac{p}{p+1},\qquad Dc_2(V)=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-scalar-widths"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-scalar-widths</summary>

*Proof.* For all sufficiently large $e$ set $$s_e=\max(d_{e-1},d'_{e-1})+1.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:width-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:width-bounds">[eq:width-bounds]</a> and <a href="#eq:canonical-width-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-width-comparison">[eq:canonical-width-comparison]</a> show that these are strictly increasing and $s_e\leq\min(d_e,d'_e)$. Work with the three rows for $E=\mathcal O_V$. At page $s_e$ all three rows are periodic modulo $M_e$, and each outer row has a single entry per period. The bottom entry is at $0$, by Equation <a href="#eq:scalar-last-width" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scalar-last-width">[eq:scalar-last-width]</a>. For the top entry, Equations <a href="#eq:top-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:top-endpoint">[eq:top-endpoint]</a> and <a href="#eq:canonical-scalar-ratio" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-scalar-ratio">[eq:canonical-scalar-ratio]</a> associate to a top weight $i$ the scalar ratio $t=1-i-h=q-i$. Its entry is therefore at $q\pmod{M_e}$.

As the page runs from $s_e$ through $s_{e+1}$, the only outer drops are those with last-width index $e$. The bottom drops, of length $d_e$, consume middle-row dimensions at $$-d_e+jM_e,\qquad p\nmid j.$$ The top drops, of length $d'_e$, consume middle-row dimensions at $$q+d'_e+jM_e,\qquad p\nmid j;$$ here replacing $j$ by $-j$ accounts for the sign in the top ratio. Put $$A_e=-d_e,\qquad B_e=q+d'_e.$$ The two lists, including their multiplicities when they meet, are thus $A_e+jM_e$ and $B_e+jM_e$, with $p\nmid j$.

We spell out the rank count also when the two centers coincide modulo $M_e$. Fix a residue $a\pmod{M_e}$ and let $m(a)\in\{0,1,2\}$ be its multiplicity in the multiset $\{A_e,B_e\}$. Its initial middle-row rank $n(a)$ is constant on all $p$ lifts modulo $pM_e$. Each of the $m(a)$ drop lists omits exactly one lift, namely its unshifted center modulo $pM_e$. At most two lifts are omitted.

<div class="samepage">

For example, when $p=5$ and $m(a)=2$, two distinct omitted lifts can be pictured as follows: $$\begin{array}{c|ccccc}
 & a&a+M_e&a+2M_e&a+3M_e&a+4M_e\\
 \text{first list}&\circ&\bullet&\bullet&\bullet&\bullet\\
 \text{second list}&\bullet&\bullet&\circ&\bullet&\bullet
\end{array}$$ A filled dot consumes one rank; a circle marks that list’s unshifted lift. The omitted lifts are illustrative. If they coincide, their column is omitted twice and every other column receives both drops. Multiplicities are always added.

</div>

In general, since $p>2$, some lift receives all $m(a)$ drops. Nonnegativity of its final rank gives $n(a)\geq m(a)$. Summing over $a$, and then using Equations <a href="#eq:periodic-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:periodic-euler">[eq:periodic-euler]</a> and <a href="#eq:scalar-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scalar-euler">[eq:scalar-euler]</a>, gives $$2\leq\sum_{a\bmod M_e}n(a)
 =2-M_e\gamma\leq2.$$ Consequently $\gamma=0$, so $Dc_2(V)=0$, and $n(a)=m(a)$ for every residue.

<a id="eq:residue-swap"></a>
After subtracting the drop lists, the final rank at each lift is exactly the number of lists omitting that lift. Thus at page $s_{e+1}$ the middle row is described by the same two-element multiset $\{A_e,B_e\}$, now modulo $M_{e+1}=pM_e$. Applying the just-proved description at index $e+1$ gives the multiset equality $$\label{eq:residue-swap}
 \{-d_e,q+d'_e\}
 =\{-d_{e+1},q+d'_{e+1}\}\pmod{M_{e+1}}.$$ The entries $-d_e$ and $-d_{e+1}$ are distinct modulo $M_{e+1}$ because $0<d_e<d_{e+1}<M_{e+1}$. Therefore the matching in Equation <a href="#eq:residue-swap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:residue-swap">[eq:residue-swap]</a> must be the swapped matching, even if a multiset initially has repeated entries. In particular $$d_{e+1}+q+d'_e\equiv0\pmod{M_{e+1}}.$$ For large $e$ its left side is positive and, by $d_{e+1}<M_{e+1}$ and $d'_e\leq d_e+C_0<M_e+C_0$, is less than $2M_{e+1}$. Hence <a id="eq:width-swap-equality"></a>$$\label{eq:width-swap-equality}
 d_{e+1}+q+d'_e=M_{e+1}.$$ Divide by $M_{e+1}=pM_e$ and let $e\to\infty$. Equation <a href="#eq:canonical-width-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:canonical-width-comparison">[eq:canonical-width-comparison]</a> gives $d'_e/M_{e+1}\to a_*/p$, while $d_{e+1}/M_{e+1}\to a_*$. Thus $a_*+a_*/p=1$, which proves Equation <a href="#eq:limiting-width" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limiting-width">[eq:limiting-width]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:laurent"></a>
### Laurent cohomology of vector bundles

<a id="eq:laurent-width-input"></a>
<a id="eq:phase-constants"></a>
We retain the smooth threefold $V$, the cycle $D$, and the notation of the preceding sections. In particular, $$\label{eq:laurent-width-input}
 M_e=rp^e,\qquad \frac{d_e}{M_e}\longrightarrow
 a_*:=\frac{p}{p+1},\qquad c_2(V)\cdot D=0.$$ The line bundle $\mathcal O_V(M_eD)$ is trivial through width $d_e$. Choose, once and for all, real numbers $$\label{eq:phase-constants}
 1-\frac2p<c_0<c<2a_*-1.$$ Such choices exist since $$2a_*-1-\left(1-\frac2p\right)=\frac{2}{p(p+1)}>0.$$ As $p\ge5$, we also have $1/2<c_0<c<a_*$. Put $u_e=\lfloor cM_e\rfloor$. The lower bound on $c_0$ permits the use of only first and second tensor powers in Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-bound">19</a>; the upper bound on $c$ leaves the precision required for matching jets in Theorem <a href="#thm:laurent-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:laurent-vanishing">20</a>.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Concentration of leading jets

The first step restricts the positions at which a long jet of a semistable bundle can have a nonzero leading term.

<a id="stmt-lem-phase-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:phase-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *Let $G$ be a vector bundle that is semistable of slope zero for the degree $c_1(-)\cdot H\cdot D$. There is a constant $A_G$ such that, for every sufficiently large $e$, $$g^0_{\lfloor c_0M_e\rfloor}(i;G)>0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(i,M_e\mathbb Z)\le A_G.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-phase-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-phase-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Write $s=\lfloor c_0M_e\rfloor$. For large $e$ we have $s\le d_e$. A unit of $\mathcal O(M_eD)|_{sD}$ translates the upper weight of a jet by $M_e$. We can therefore replace $i$ by a centered representative $v$, with $|v|\le M_e/2$, without changing the existence of its leading class.

Suppose that no uniform bound on $|v|$ exists. Passing to a sequence, we may assume that $v\to+\infty$ or $v\to-\infty$. In the first case $s>v$ for large $e$. The jet maps to a global section of $G(vD)/G$ and retains its nonzero leading term. Its pole order tends to infinity, contrary to Proposition <a href="#prop:polar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polar-bounds">12</a>.

<a id="eq:phase-tensor-choice"></a>
In the second case choose a real number $\lambda$ such that <a id="eq:phase-lambda"></a>$$\label{eq:phase-lambda}
 \frac{1-c_0}{2}<\lambda<\frac1p,
 \qquad p\lambda>\frac12.$$ The interval is nonempty by <a href="#eq:phase-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-constants">[eq:phase-constants]</a>. Since the periods increase by the factor $p$, we can choose $j\le e$ with $$\lambda\le\frac{|v|}{M_j}\le p\lambda.$$ Here $j\to\infty$ as $|v|\to\infty$. After a subsequence the ratios tend to a number $y\in[\lambda,p\lambda]$. There is an integer $m\in\{1,2\}$ for which $$\label{eq:phase-tensor-choice}
 1-c_0<my<1.$$ Indeed, if $y>1-c_0$, take $m=1$; then $y\le p\lambda<1$. Otherwise take $m=2$. Its lower bound follows from $2y\ge2\lambda>1-c_0$, and its upper bound follows from $2y\le2(1-c_0)<4/p<1$.

Restrict the original jet to width $\lfloor c_0M_j\rfloor$, take its $m$th tensor power, and multiply by a unit section of $\mathcal O(M_jD)$ to that width. Tensoring keeps at least the same width: in a difference of two tensor products, every summand contains a factor from the lower lattice. The resulting jet is a jet of $G^{\otimes m}$ at upper weight $$w=M_j+mv.$$ By <a href="#eq:phase-tensor-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-tensor-choice">[eq:phase-tensor-choice]</a>, $w\to+\infty$ and $w<\lfloor c_0M_j\rfloor$ with a margin tending to infinity. It therefore gives a global polar part of $G^{\otimes m}$.

This polar part has unbounded actual order. At some component of $D$ an original coefficient has pole ratio greater than $v-1$. The corresponding pure tensor coefficient, after multiplication by the unit, has pole ratio greater than $M_j+m(v-1)$. This tends to infinity. Proposition <a href="#prop:polar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polar-bounds">12</a> applies to both $G$ and $G\otimes G$, giving the required contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### A vanishing criterion

We use $\widehat E$ for the completion of a bundle $E$ along $D$, and $E_{\mathcal R}=\widehat E\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal O}}\mathcal R$. The finite-cover, bounded-pole interpretation of these sheaves is the one used in the jet construction.

<a id="stmt-thm-laurent-vanishing"></a>
<div id="thm:laurent-vanishing" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20**. *For the prepared data and periods fixed above, let $E$ be a vector bundle on $V$ with the following properties:*

1.  *$\chi(P_i^1(E))=0$ for every integer $i$.*

2.  *For $J=E,E^!$, the spaces $H^0(V,J(nD)/J)$ have dimensions bounded independently of the positive integer $n$.*

3.  *For $J=E,E^!$ there is a constant $A_J$ such that $$g^0_{u_e}(i;J)>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
     \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(i,M_e\mathbb Z)\le A_J$$ for all sufficiently large $e$.*

*Then global sections of $E_{\mathcal R}$ form a finite-dimensional $k$-vector space. Every Laurent Čech $1$-cocycle on a finite affine cover adapted to the bundles and the divisor has an actual Laurent primitive. The same assertion holds after refining such a cover.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-laurent-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-laurent-vanishing</summary>

*Proof.* We separate the argument into the endpoint patterns, their drops, the middle row, and convergence.

*Endpoint patterns.* For $e$ large, $u_e\le d_e$, so the page at width $u_e$ is periodic modulo $M_e$. By (iii), the bottom row can be supported only at a fixed bounded set of centered positions. At each such position the descending sequence of leading images stabilizes as the width tends to infinity. The stable-image statement of Lemma <a href="#lem:page-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:page-calculus">14</a> realizes every vector in the stabilized image by a global completed section at that cut. Consequently, for all sufficiently large $e$ there is one fixed bottom pattern, at positions $v$ with fixed multiplicities, repeated by translations by $M_e$. Duality gives a fixed top pattern at positions $w$. We enlarge a constant $A$ so that all these $v,w$ lie in $[-A,A]$.

This already proves finite-dimensionality of global Laurent sections. A nonzero section has a least upper cut $i$: its poles are bounded, and the filtration is separated toward negative infinity. Its nonzero leading class belongs to $g^0_{u_e}(i;E)$ for every large $e$. With $i$ fixed and $M_e\to\infty$, condition (iii) forces $i$ to be an unshifted position of the fixed pattern. There are only finitely many such cuts, and each leading quotient is finite-dimensional. These quotients give a finite filtration of the space of global sections. The same observation applies to $E^!$.

<a id="eq:drop-range"></a>
*Endpoint drops.* By the middle-row recurrence, a bottom drop of length $s$ at $v+jM_e$ removes middle-row rank at $v-s+jM_e$; a top drop at $w+jM_e$ removes rank at $w+s+jM_e$. To determine these losses over the next period, we must control their lengths, with multiplicities, independently of $j$ whenever $p\nmid j$. Consider a bottom position $i=v+jM_e$. If $p\mid j$, the next period $M_{e+1}=pM_e$ retains exactly the same multiplicity there. Monotonicity therefore permits no drop between pages $u_e$ and $u_{e+1}$. If $p\nmid j$, all of this multiplicity disappears by page $u_{e+1}$. We claim that its drops occur at lengths $$\label{eq:drop-range}
 d_e\le s\le M_e+C$$ for a fixed $C$, and that their multiplicities at each length are independent of $j$ among integers prime to $p$.

<a id="eq:normalized-jet-overlap"></a>
There is no drop before length $d_e$, since translation by a unit of $\mathcal O(M_eD)|_{d_eD}$ identifies the leading images with those at the unshifted stable position $v$. Choose local formal unit lifts $t_\lambda$ of this trivializing section of $\mathcal O(M_eD)|_{d_eD}$, and write on overlaps $$\frac{t_\lambda}{t_\mu}=1+b_{\lambda\mu},
 \qquad b_{\lambda\mu}\in\mathcal I_D^{d_e}\widehat{\mathcal O}_V,
 \qquad \mathcal I_D=\mathcal O_V(-D).$$ Fix a constant $C$ for the moment and a width $$d_e<k\le M_e+C.$$ For large $e$, $k<2d_e$ by <a href="#eq:laurent-width-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laurent-width-input">[eq:laurent-width-input]</a>. Normalize local representatives $\sigma_\lambda$ of a width-$k$ jet at $v+jM_e$ by putting $\alpha_\lambda=\sigma_\lambda/t_\lambda^j$. These are local sections of $\widehat{E(vD)}$ and satisfy $$\label{eq:normalized-jet-overlap}
 \alpha_\mu=(1+jb_{\lambda\mu})\alpha_\lambda
 \pmod{\mathcal I_D^k\widehat{E(vD)}}.$$ Indeed $(1+b_{\lambda\mu})^j=1+jb_{\lambda\mu}$ to this precision, for positive or negative $j$ alike, because $k<2d_e$. In particular, the cochain $\alpha$ glues to width $d_e$.

We next match $\alpha$ to a single global completed section $\beta$ in that untwisted lattice through width $k-d_e$. Every completed section and every formal lattice map restricts to the actual algebraic finite jets of Equation <a href="#eq:jet-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jet-quotient">[eq:jet-quotient]</a>: on each affine intersection this is the identity $\widehat A/(f^s)=A/(f^s)$. These restrictions commute with the Čech maps and with projection to leading layers. Start with $\beta_{<0}=0$. After matching the first $n$ depths, the sum $\beta_{<n}$ of the completed global sections already chosen satisfies $$\alpha-\beta_{<n}\in C_{v-n}^0(E),\qquad
 \partial(\alpha-\beta_{<n})\in C_{v-d_e}^1(E).$$ The second inclusion persists because each subtracted section is global. Thus, for $n<k-d_e$, the residual is a finite jet at upper weight $v-n$ of width at least $d_e-n$. The inequalities <a id="eq:matching-margin"></a>$$\label{eq:matching-margin}
 d_e-n\ge 2d_e-M_e-O(1)>u_e,
 \qquad k-d_e<M_e/2-O(1)$$ hold with margins growing linearly in $M_e$, by $c<2a_*-1$ and $a_*>1/2$. Thus $v-n$ is centered, and condition (iii) says that its leading image is either zero or one of the unshifted stabilized images. In the latter case lift it to a completed global section and subtract. A finite induction constructs the asserted $\beta$.

If $p\nmid j,j'$, put $q=j'/j$ in the prime field and set $$\alpha'=q\alpha+(1-q)\beta.$$ Since $\beta$ is global, Equation <a href="#eq:normalized-jet-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-jet-overlap">[eq:normalized-jet-overlap]</a> gives $$\alpha'_\mu-(1+j'b_{\lambda\mu})\alpha'_\lambda
 =j'(1-q)b_{\lambda\mu}(\alpha_\lambda-\beta)
 \pmod{\mathcal I_D^k\widehat{E(vD)}}.$$ The right side vanishes: its two factors have depths $d_e$ and $k-d_e$. Retwisting by $t_\lambda^{j'}$ therefore gives a width-$k$ jet at $v+j'M_e$. It has the same normalized leading term, since $k-d_e\ge1$. Also $\alpha'-\beta=q(\alpha-\beta)$, so the same $\beta$ reverses the construction with $q^{-1}$. The normalized leading-image subspaces are consequently equal at every width under consideration. Their ranks, and hence their drops at each length, are independent of $j$ prime to $p$.

For $j=1$, property (ii) kills the entire leading image by width $M_e+C$ once $C>A$ is sufficiently large. Indeed this width is at least the upper weight $M_e+v$, so a surviving leading jet would give a global polar part of order tending to infinity. The increasing spaces in (ii) stabilize and forbid such polar parts. Since $u_{e+1}>M_e+C$ for large $e$, this proves <a href="#eq:drop-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:drop-range">[eq:drop-range]</a> and the complete drop rule. The identical argument for $E^!$, followed by the duality of Lemma <a href="#lem:page-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:page-calculus">14</a>, gives the corresponding top-row rule.

<a id="eq:middle-distribution"></a>
<a id="eq:drop-bands"></a>
<a id="eq:fixed-weight-vanishing"></a>
*The middle row.* We use the nonnegativity and Euler comparison from Proposition <a href="#prop:scalar-widths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scalar-widths">18</a>, now with a multiset of drop positions. Let $T_e$ be the multiset of the numbers $v-s$ for the bottom drops and $w+s$ for the top drops. Each is counted with its drop multiplicity for one translating integer prime to $p$. Its total size is the sum of the ranks of the two fixed endpoint patterns. Put $H_e(i)=g^1_{u_e}(i;E)$. The middle-row recurrence in Lemma <a href="#lem:page-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:page-calculus">14</a> gives $$\label{eq:middle-distribution}
\begin{split}
 H_{e+1}(i)=H_e(i)
 &-\#\{t\in T_e:t\equiv i\pmod{M_e}\}\\
 &+\#\{t\in T_e:t\equiv i\pmod{pM_e}\}.
\end{split}$$ The last term restores the unshifted copies, which do not drop. All members of $T_e$ lie in the two intervals $$\label{eq:drop-bands}
 [-M_e-O(1),-d_e+O(1)]
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 [d_e-O(1),M_e+O(1)].$$ Each interval has length less than $M_e$ for large $e$. For any fixed residue modulo $M_e$, these intervals can occupy at most two of its lifts modulo $pM_e$. Since $p\ge5$, some lift is missed. Nonnegativity of $H_{e+1}$ at that lift and periodicity of $H_e$ show that $$H_e(i)\ge\#\{t\in T_e:t\equiv i\pmod{M_e}\}.$$ Summing over a period gives equality of the two sides, by the Euler identity in Lemma <a href="#lem:page-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:page-calculus">14</a> and property (i). The inequality is therefore equality at every residue. Equation <a href="#eq:middle-distribution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:middle-distribution">[eq:middle-distribution]</a> now says that $H_{e+1}$ is exactly the distribution of $T_e$ modulo $pM_e$. For each fixed integer $i$, the bands <a href="#eq:drop-bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:drop-bands">[eq:drop-bands]</a> and $d_e\to\infty$ imply $$\label{eq:fixed-weight-vanishing}
 g^1_{u_e}(i;E)=0\qquad\text{for all sufficiently large }e.$$

<a id="eq:primitive-cut-bound"></a>
*Convergence of primitives.* Fixed-weight page vanishing alone does not bound the poles of successive primitives. Fix an integer upper bound $l$ for a Laurent cocycle. We will bound the source $z=x+s$ of every nonzero incoming differential of length $s$ to a middle-row weight $x\le l$ by a function $B_l(x)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:primitive-cut-bound}
 \sup_{x\le l}B_l(x)<\infty,
 \qquad B_l(x)\longrightarrow-\infty\quad(x\to-\infty).$$

At a sufficiently late period, the drop rule gives $z=v+jM_e$, $p\nmid j$, and $d_e\le s\le M_e+C$. If $j\ge2$, then $x\ge M_e-A-C$, which exceeds $l$ for all sufficiently large $e$. If $j=1$, the target must lie in the fixed finite set of integers in $[-A-C,l]$. Choose one index $e_0$, depending on $l$, beyond the thresholds for the endpoint and drop rules, so that $M_e-A-C>l$ for $e\ge e_0$ and every middle rank in this finite set is zero at page $u_{e_0}$. Equation <a href="#eq:fixed-weight-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-weight-vanishing">[eq:fixed-weight-vanishing]</a> permits this simultaneous choice. Middle ranks only decrease, so no nonzero incoming image can have $j=1$ at any later page.

Put $S_l=u_{e_0}\ge1$. Earlier lengths $s<S_l$ give $z\le x+S_l$. Every later length belongs to a period $u_e\le s<u_{e+1}$ with $e\ge e_0$; the preceding exclusions leave only $j\le-1$. In that case $z\le-M_e+A$, and hence $M_e\le-z+A$. Thus $x=z-s\ge2z-A-C$, or $$z\le\frac{x+A+C}{2}.$$ The real-valued upper bound $$B_l(x)=\max\left\{x+S_l,\frac{x+A+C}{2}\right\}
 \qquad(x\le l)$$ therefore bounds every possible source and satisfies both assertions of Equation <a href="#eq:primitive-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primitive-cut-bound">[eq:primitive-cut-bound]</a>.

Let $\gamma$ be an actual Laurent $1$-cocycle of upper cut $x\le l$. Its leading cochain belongs to every cycle space $Z_s^{x,1}$. By <a href="#eq:fixed-weight-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-weight-vanishing">[eq:fixed-weight-vanishing]</a>, it eventually belongs to the boundary space $U_s^{x,1}$. More precisely, the page construction identifies $$U_{s+1}^{x,1}/U_s^{x,1}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(d_s:E_s^{x+s,0}\longrightarrow E_s^{x,1}\bigr),$$ where $E_s^{i,a}=Z_s^{i,a}/U_s^{i,a}$. Thus $U_s^{x,1}$ stops increasing once $x+s>B_l(x)$. By its definition, each increase from a source cut $z=x+s$ has a representative primitive in $C_z^0(E)$; the initial space $U_1^{x,1}$ uses $C_x^0(E)$. Consequently the leading cochain of $\gamma$ has a primitive of cut at most $\max\{x,B_l(x)\}$. Subtract that primitive’s coboundary. The new cocycle has strictly smaller upper cut. Repeating at successive descending cuts gives primitives whose upper cuts tend to negative infinity by <a href="#eq:primitive-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primitive-cut-bound">[eq:primitive-cut-bound]</a>. Their sum converges in a single bounded-pole completed lattice. The differential is continuous, and the filtration is separated, so its coboundary is $\gamma$. All arguments use finite covers by affine charts and their affine intersections, and remain valid after refinement. This proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Cartier algebra on the Laurent charts

We specify the module structures before applying the criterion to exact forms. Write $F$ for absolute Frobenius and $\Omega=\Omega_V^1$. Let $B^1,Z^1\subset F_*\Omega$ be the sheaves of exact and closed algebraic one-forms, with the Frobenius module structure.

<a id="stmt-lem-laurent-cartier-algebra"></a>
<div id="lem:laurent-cartier-algebra" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cartier-sequences"></a>
<a id="eq:frobenius-duality"></a>
**Lemma 21**. *On a smooth affine chart with étale coordinates $x_1,x_2,x_3$ and a local equation $f$ for $D$, put $R=\widehat A[1/f]$. The monomials $x^I=x_1^{I_1}x_2^{I_2}x_3^{I_3}$, $0\le I_j<p$, form a basis of $R$ over $R^p$. Ordinary differentials and the Cartier isomorphism extend to this ring. The algebraic bundles $B^1,Z^1$ and their defining exact sequences extend to the corresponding Laurent exact and closed forms, with their Frobenius module structure. In particular, $$\label{eq:cartier-sequences}
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal O_V\longrightarrow F_*\mathcal O_V
   \longrightarrow B^1\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 0\longrightarrow B^1\longrightarrow Z^1
   \xrightarrow{C}\Omega\longrightarrow0$$ are sequences of vector bundles and remain exact on Laurent charts. The images and kernels in the Frobenius de Rham complex are locally free, as are the relevant quotients. Moreover, $$\label{eq:frobenius-duality}
 (F_*\mathcal O_V)^\vee\otimes\omega_V\simeq F_*\omega_V.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-laurent-cartier-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-laurent-cartier-algebra</summary>

*Proof.* The algebraic calculation is the $p$-basis form of Cartier’s theorem (Cartier 1957, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[cartier1957](#ref-cartier1957)]; see also (Katz 1970, Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[katz1970](#ref-katz1970)]. We first check that its finite basis survives the completion and localization used here. For an étale algebra the relative Frobenius square is a base change square. One can check this because the relative Frobenius is étale and induces isomorphisms on geometric fibers. The polynomial-coordinate monomial basis over $p$th powers therefore gives the asserted basis for $A$ over $A^p$. The field $k$ is perfect.

Complete the $p$-power subring along $f^p$ and use this finite basis. The $f$-adic and $f^p$-adic topologies on $A$ coincide, and Frobenius identifies the completion of $A^p$ with $(\widehat A)^p$. The basis thus persists after completion and then after inverting $f$; in the latter step, $1/f=f^{p-1}/f^p$.

Every $k$-derivation kills $p$th powers. Since each element has a unique finite expansion in the displayed basis with coefficients in $R^p$, its differential is determined by ordinary differentiation of those monomials. Conversely the coordinate derivations are defined by that rule. Hence $\Omega^1_{R/k}$ is free on $dx_j$. There is no additional continuity assumption on differentials.

In one variable, the de Rham complex decomposes into the monomials of degrees less than $p$; its cohomology has representatives $1$ in degree zero and $x^{p-1}dx$ in degree one. Tensoring the three one-variable complexes proves local freeness of images, kernels, and quotients, and proves the inverse Cartier rule $$f\longmapsto f^p,\qquad
 df\longmapsto[f^{p-1}df].$$ The rule is intrinsic. Leibniz holds directly, while additivity for differentials follows modulo exact forms by differentiating the mixed terms of the integral polynomial $((X+Y)^p-X^p-Y^p)/p$ and reducing modulo $p$. Thus the local isomorphisms glue.

More explicitly, the scalar extension of the algebraic Frobenius complex to a Laurent chart is the actual Laurent de Rham complex with Frobenius action, through $$R\otimes_A F_*\Omega_A^\bullet\longrightarrow
 F_*\Omega_R^\bullet,
 \qquad a\otimes\eta\longmapsto a^p\eta.$$ The monomial and coordinate-wedge bases make this an isomorphism. This proves the assertions about <a href="#eq:cartier-sequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartier-sequences">[eq:cartier-sequences]</a>. Finally, the pairing obtained by Cartier on top forms is perfect: in coordinates it extracts the coefficient of $x_1^{p-1}x_2^{p-1}x_3^{p-1}dx_1\wedge dx_2\wedge dx_3$. It gives <a href="#eq:frobenius-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frobenius-duality">[eq:frobenius-duality]</a> and is intrinsic by the same Cartier rule. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Application to one-forms and exact forms

<a id="stmt-cor-form-vanishing"></a>
<div id="cor:form-vanishing" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22**. *Every Laurent Čech $1$-cocycle with values in $\Omega$, $B^1$, or $Z^1$ has a Laurent primitive. Global Laurent one-forms form a finite-dimensional $k$-vector space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-form-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-form-vanishing</summary>

*Proof.* For $E=\Omega$, both $E$ and $E^!=T_V\otimes\omega_V$ are semistable of slope zero by Proposition <a href="#prop:strong-semistability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:strong-semistability">9</a> and the canonical intersection identities. Proposition <a href="#prop:polar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polar-bounds">12</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-bound">19</a> give conditions (ii) and (iii) of Theorem <a href="#thm:laurent-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:laurent-vanishing">20</a>; passing from width $\lfloor c_0M_e\rfloor$ to $u_e$ can only decrease the leading images. Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch gives condition (i). Indeed the degree-two terms in $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\Omega)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_V)$ are linear combinations of $K_V^2$ and $c_2(V)$, both killed by intersection with $D$, and every term involving $D^2$ is also zero.

For $E=B^1$, the first sequence in <a href="#eq:cartier-sequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartier-sequences">[eq:cartier-sequences]</a> gives condition (i). By projection formula, a single cell for $F_*\mathcal O_V$ has the Euler characteristic of $p$ successive scalar cells; each is zero by Proposition <a href="#prop:scalar-widths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scalar-widths">18</a>. Frobenius changes the $k$-module action but not these finite dimensions.

<a id="eq:exact-form-inclusions"></a>
There are subbundle inclusions with locally free quotients $$\label{eq:exact-form-inclusions}
 B^1%
  \mathrel{%
    \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=21AA}%
    \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow%
    \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
  }%
F_*\Omega,
 \qquad
 (B^1)^!%
  \mathrel{%
    \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=21AA}%
    \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow%
    \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
  }%
F_*\omega_V.$$ The first follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:laurent-cartier-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:laurent-cartier-algebra">21</a>; the second follows by dualizing the first sequence in <a href="#eq:cartier-sequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartier-sequences">[eq:cartier-sequences]</a> and using <a href="#eq:frobenius-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frobenius-duality">[eq:frobenius-duality]</a>. For a subbundle $E\subset F_*G$, the injection remains injective on every quotient $E(nD)/E$. Projection formula embeds that quotient in $F_*(G(pnD)/G)$. The polar bounds for $G=\Omega$ and $G=\omega_V$ therefore give condition (ii) for both bundles in <a href="#eq:exact-form-inclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-form-inclusions">[eq:exact-form-inclusions]</a>. All these bounds concern fixed bundles and their first Frobenius pushforwards. The growing index $e$ below changes the jet period, not the bundle in the polar-part estimate.

The same subbundle property gives condition (iii), with a small precision margin that is useful to make explicit. A width-$u_e$ jet of $E\subset F_*G$ with a nonzero leading term maps to a width-$pu_e$ jet of $G$ at upper weight $pi$. Its leading term is nonzero in the top $p$ ordinary layers. Choose its first nonzero ordinary layer, at a weight $a$ with $|a-pi|<p$. It has width at least $pu_e-p$.
