Since $c>c_0$ and $M_{e+1}=pM_e$, for all sufficiently large $e$ we have $$pu_e-p\ge\lfloor c_0M_{e+1}\rfloor.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-bound">19</a> at period $M_{e+1}$ implies that $a$, and hence $pi$, is at bounded distance from $M_{e+1}\mathbb Z$. Dividing by $p$ gives the required bounded distance from $M_e\mathbb Z$.

Theorem <a href="#thm:laurent-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:laurent-vanishing">20</a> now applies to $\Omega$ and $B^1$, including finite-dimensionality for the former. For a $Z^1$ cocycle, first solve its image in $\Omega$ and lift the resulting local primitives through Cartier. After subtraction the remaining cocycle lies in $B^1$ and can also be solved. These lifts exist on affine Laurent charts by Lemma <a href="#lem:laurent-cartier-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:laurent-cartier-algebra">21</a>; refining the finite cover if necessary preserves every preceding argument. This proves the assertion for $Z^1$. ◻

</details>

The next section uses both conclusions: cocycle vanishing supplies compatible closed connection forms, and finite-dimensionality permits a global adjustment that makes those forms Cartier-fixed.

<a id="sec:cartier"></a>
### Cartier descent and the final degree contradiction

<a id="eq:laurent-cartier-sequence"></a>
We work on the Zariski topological space $|D|$, with $$\widehat{\mathcal O}=\widehat{\mathcal O}_V,
 \qquad \mathcal R=\widehat{\mathcal O}_V(*D).$$ Here and throughout this section, a line bundle means a locally free module of rank one for the indicated sheaf of rings, in this Zariski topology. We write $\Omega^j_{\mathcal R}$ for the actual Laurent differential forms, and $\mathcal B^1\subset\mathcal Z^1\subset\Omega^1_{\mathcal R}$ for the additive sheaves of exact and closed one-forms. Lemma <a href="#lem:laurent-cartier-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:laurent-cartier-algebra">21</a> identifies these with the Laurent extensions of the algebraic Frobenius modules. In particular, there is an exact sequence of additive sheaves $$0\longrightarrow\mathcal B^1\longrightarrow\mathcal Z^1
   \xrightarrow{\ C\ }\Omega^1_{\mathcal R}\longrightarrow0,
 \label{eq:laurent-cartier-sequence}$$ where $C$ is inverse-Frobenius-semilinear for the ordinary scalar structures: $$C(a^p\alpha)=aC(\alpha).$$ The distinction between these scalar structures and the Frobenius-module structures is essential in using the preceding cohomology calculation. Corollary <a href="#cor:form-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:form-vanishing">22</a> supplies the Laurent cocycle vanishings and finite-dimensionality of global one-forms that we use to make $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(|D|,\mathcal R)$ $p$-divisible. Orders along the components of $D$ then let intersection with $H$ descend to a nonzero integer degree on that same group. These two properties are incompatible.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Global Cartier adjustment

<a id="stmt-lem-cartier-adjustment"></a>
<div id="lem:cartier-adjustment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *Every global Laurent one-form is closed. On the finite-dimensional $k$-vector space $$W=H^0(|D|,\Omega^1_{\mathcal R}),$$ Cartier is a bijective inverse-Frobenius-semilinear map, and $C-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}:W\to W$ is surjective as an additive map. Moreover, every additive $\mathcal Z^1$-valued Čech one-cocycle is a coboundary after refining its open cover.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cartier-adjustment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cartier-adjustment</summary>

*Proof.* Corollary <a href="#cor:form-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:form-vanishing">22</a> gives finite-dimensionality of $W$ and actual Laurent one-cocycle vanishing for $\mathcal B^1$ and $\mathcal Z^1$, proving the last assertion. To lift a global form through $C$, choose local lifts in $\mathcal Z^1$ using Equation <a href="#eq:laurent-cartier-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laurent-cartier-sequence">[eq:laurent-cartier-sequence]</a>. Their differences form a $\mathcal B^1$-cocycle. Subtracting a coboundary makes the lifts agree, so $$C:H^0(|D|,\mathcal Z^1)\longrightarrow W$$ is surjective. Its source is a vector subspace of $W$. Since the ground field is perfect, a semilinear map has the same dimension properties as a linear map. Finite-dimensionality therefore implies $H^0(\mathcal Z^1)=W$ and bijectivity of $C$.

For the last assertion about $C$, set $F=C^{-1}$, a bijective $p$-semilinear map. Choose coordinates on $W\simeq k^n$. Then $F(x)=A x^{[p]}$ with $A\in\operatorname{GL}_n(k)$, where $x^{[p]}$ means componentwise $p$-th powers. The polynomial map $F-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ extends to <a id="eq:semilinear-projective-map"></a>$$\Psi:\mathbb P^n\longrightarrow\mathbb P^n,
 \qquad
 [x:t]\longmapsto
 [A x^{[p]}-t^{p-1}x:t^p].
 \label{eq:semilinear-projective-map}$$ There is no base point: if $t=0$, the first coordinates vanish only when $x=0$. Also $\Psi^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^n}(1)=
\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^n}(p)$. A positive-dimensional fiber would contain a projective curve on which this line has both degree zero and positive degree, a contradiction. Hence $\Psi$ is finite. Its image is closed and has dimension $n$, so it is all of $\mathbb P^n$. The inverse image of the standard affine chart is exactly that chart, proving surjectivity of $F-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ over the algebraically closed field. The case $n=0$ is immediate. Applying $F$ to $(C-\mathop{\mathrm{id}})\beta=\eta$ gives the equivalent equation $$(F-\mathop{\mathrm{id}})\beta=-F\eta,$$ so $C-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$ is surjective as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cartier-fixed-connection"></a>
<div id="lem:cartier-fixed-connection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *Every $\mathcal R$-line bundle admits an integrable connection whose local connection one-forms are fixed by Cartier.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cartier-fixed-connection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cartier-fixed-connection</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\mathcal L$ be such a line bundle. Its frame cover can be refined by opens $U_i\cap|D|$, where $U_i\subset V$ is affine, has étale coordinates, makes $D$ principal, and trivializes the finitely many algebraic bundles in use. Quasi-compactness of $|D|$ permits a finite such cover. Its finite intersections are restrictions of affine opens, since $V$ is separated. On each intersection, a Laurent coefficient has a finite pole bound: take a finite subcover of local bounds and glue in their common completed lattice, as in Section <a href="#sec:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jets">4</a>. Taking the maximum over the finitely many coefficients gives a common upper cut for a cochain. These observations also apply after finite refinements for local Cartier lifts. Thus the cocycles below lie in the actual Laurent complexes to which the vanishing results apply.

Choose frames $e_i$ on this cover, and write $e_j=g_{ij}e_i$ on overlaps. Then $g_{ij}g_{jk}=g_{ik}$, so $$d\log g_{ij}=g_{ij}^{-1}dg_{ij}$$ is a closed additive one-cocycle. It is Cartier-fixed. Indeed, the intrinsic inverse Cartier formula gives $$C^{-1}(g^{-1}dg)
  =\bigl[g^{-p}g^{p-1}dg\bigr]=[g^{-1}dg]$$ for every Laurent unit $g$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:cartier-adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cartier-adjustment">23</a>, after refining the cover there are closed one-forms $\alpha_i$ such that $$\alpha_j-\alpha_i=d\log g_{ij}.$$ These are the connection forms of a connection defined by $\nabla e_i=\alpha_i\otimes e_i$. Their closedness is precisely its integrability, since the bundle has rank one.

The forms $C\alpha_i-\alpha_i$ agree on overlaps and hence define a global form $\eta\in W$. Choose $\beta\in W$ with $(C-\mathop{\mathrm{id}})\beta=\eta$, using Lemma <a href="#lem:cartier-adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cartier-adjustment">23</a>. Replace every $\alpha_i$ by $\alpha_i-\beta$. This preserves their differences and their closedness, and now $C\alpha_i=\alpha_i$ for all $i$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Horizontal sections over a Laurent chart

We give the local descent calculation, including its compatibility with restriction. This is the algebra underlying Cartier descent (Katz 1970, Theorem 5.1 and formulas (5.1.2)–(5.1.7)) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[katz1970](#ref-katz1970)]; the formulas below apply directly to our Laurent rings by their retained $p$-basis, without a finite-type hypothesis on those rings.

<a id="eq:cartier-pbasis"></a>
Take a smooth affine chart $U=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A\subset V$ with étale coordinates $x_1,x_2,x_3$, and let $f$ be an equation of $D$ on it. Put $$B=\varprojlim_n A/(f^n),\qquad R=B[1/f],\qquad S=R^p.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:laurent-cartier-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:laurent-cartier-algebra">21</a> gives $$R=\bigoplus_{0\le I_1,I_2,I_3<p}Sx^I,
 \qquad
 \Omega^1_{R/k}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^3R\,dx_i.
 \label{eq:cartier-pbasis}$$ Write $\partial_i$ for the coordinate derivations. They commute, kill $S$, and satisfy $\partial_i^p=0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-cartier-curvature"></a>
<div id="lem:cartier-curvature" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:horizontal-relations"></a>
**Lemma 25** (The rank-one curvature calculation). *If $\alpha=\sum_i a_i\,dx_i$ is closed and $C\alpha=\alpha$, then the connection operators $$D_i=\partial_i+a_i:R\longrightarrow R$$ commute and satisfy $$D_i^p=0,
 \qquad [D_i,x_j]=\delta_{ij}.
 \label{eq:horizontal-relations}$$ Here a ring element in an operator formula denotes multiplication by that element.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cartier-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cartier-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* If $C\alpha=\sum_i b_i\,dx_i$, the $p$-basis Cartier calculation represents $\alpha$ in the form $$\alpha=dc+\sum_i b_i^p x_i^{p-1}\,dx_i
 \qquad(c\in R).$$ Consequently <a id="eq:cartier-coefficient"></a>$$\partial_i^{p-1}a_i
   =(p-1)!\,b_i^p=-b_i^p.
 \label{eq:cartier-coefficient}$$ Exact terms disappear because $\partial_i^p c=0$, and $b_i^p$ is constant for every coordinate derivation. The Cartier-fixed condition therefore says $a_i^p+\partial_i^{p-1}a_i=0$.

<a id="eq:rank-one-pcurvature"></a>
We recall the operator identity giving its significance. For a derivation $\partial$ with $\partial^p=0$ and an element $a\in R$, $$(\partial+a)^p=a^p+\partial^{p-1}a.
 \label{eq:rank-one-pcurvature}$$ First, the left side commutes with multiplication by any $b\in R$: in characteristic $p$, its commutator is the $p$-fold iterated commutator, namely multiplication by $\partial^p b=0$. It is therefore multiplication by its value on $1$. Over the integers, successive use of the product rule expands $(\partial+a)^p(1)$ as a sum indexed by set partitions of $\{1,\ldots,p\}$, a block of size $j$ contributing $\partial^{j-1}a$. This formula follows by adjoining the last label either as a singleton or to one existing block. A cyclic permutation of the $p$ labels has orbits of size $p$ on these partitions, except for the discrete partition and the partition with one block. To check the exceptions, in an invariant partition a block is either the whole set or has an orbit of $p$ members, in which case all blocks are singletons. The summands have the same value throughout an orbit, so only $a^p$ and $\partial^{p-1}a$ survive in characteristic $p$. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:rank-one-pcurvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-one-pcurvature">[eq:rank-one-pcurvature]</a> and hence $D_i^p=0$. Finally, $$[D_i,D_j]=\partial_i a_j-\partial_j a_i=0$$ by closedness, and the commutator with $x_j$ follows directly from the Leibniz rule. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-horizontal-expansion"></a>
<div id="lem:horizontal-expansion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:horizontal-descent"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Horizontal expansion). *Let $M$ be a free rank-one $R$-module with a connection whose operators satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:horizontal-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-relations">[eq:horizontal-relations]</a> and commute. Its horizontal module $$H_M=\bigcap_i\ker(D_i:M\to M)$$ is a finite projective rank-one $S$-module, and multiplication gives an isomorphism $$R\otimes_S H_M\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }M.
 \label{eq:horizontal-descent}$$ Formation of $H_M$ commutes with restriction to a smaller Laurent chart retaining the coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-horizontal-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-horizontal-expansion</summary>

<a id="eq:horizontal-projector"></a>
*Proof.* Define $S$-linear operators $$P_i=\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\frac{(-1)^n}{n!}x_i^nD_i^n,
 \qquad P=P_1P_2P_3.
 \label{eq:horizontal-projector}$$ The multiplication operator $x_i^n$ is placed on the left. All factorials here are invertible in $k$. The commutator relation gives a telescoping sum $$D_iP_i=\frac{(-1)^{p-1}}{(p-1)!}x_i^{p-1}D_i^p=0.$$ Also $P_i$ is the identity on $\ker D_i$, and distinct $P_i$’s commute with each other and with $D_j$ for $j\ne i$. Hence $P$ is an idempotent with image $H_M$.

<a id="eq:horizontal-taylor-one"></a>
For one coordinate, the binomial identity gives $$\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}\frac{x_i^n}{n!}P_iD_i^n=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_M.
 \label{eq:horizontal-taylor-one}$$ Indeed, after substituting Equation <a href="#eq:horizontal-projector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-projector">[eq:horizontal-projector]</a>, the coefficient of $x_i^rD_i^r$, for $0\le r<p$, is $$\sum_{n+j=r}\frac{(-1)^j}{n!j!}=\begin{cases}1&r=0,\\0&r>0.
 \end{cases}$$ All terms with $r\ge p$ vanish by $D_i^p=0$. Multiplying Equation <a href="#eq:horizontal-taylor-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-taylor-one">[eq:horizontal-taylor-one]</a> over the three coordinates gives the explicit expansion <a id="eq:horizontal-taylor"></a>$$m=\sum_{0\le I_j<p}x^Ih_I,
 \qquad h_I=\frac1{I!}P(D^I m)\in H_M.
 \label{eq:horizontal-taylor}$$ Here $I!=I_1!I_2!I_3!$ and $D^I=D_1^{I_1}D_2^{I_2}D_3^{I_3}$. The coefficients are unique: for $h\in H_M$, $$P D^J(x^Ih)=\begin{cases}I!h&I=J,\\0&I\ne J,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad 0\le I_i,J_i<p.$$ This follows by differentiation and the same binomial sum, which makes $P(x^Kh)=0$ for every nonzero $K$ in that range. Together with Equation <a href="#eq:cartier-pbasis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartier-pbasis">[eq:cartier-pbasis]</a>, this proves Equation <a href="#eq:horizontal-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-descent">[eq:horizontal-descent]</a>.

As an $S$-module, $M$ is free of rank $p^3$. The image of the idempotent $P$ is therefore finite projective. The extension $S\subset R$ is finite free and faithfully flat; Equation <a href="#eq:horizontal-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-descent">[eq:horizontal-descent]</a> shows that this projective module has rank one.

For restriction, let $R\to R'$ be the homomorphism of Laurent chart rings and set $S'=(R')^p$. The shared monomial bases give an isomorphism $$S'\otimes_S R\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }R'.$$ The restricted module is consequently $S'\otimes_S M$, its operators are $1\otimes D_i$, and its projector is $1\otimes P$. Taking the image of a split idempotent commutes with every scalar extension, so its horizontal module is $S'\otimes_S H_M$. This argument does not require a separate flatness assertion for the restriction homomorphism. Since horizontality means $\nabla m=0$, the construction is also independent of the coordinate system and the chosen line frame. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The formal Zariski topology and Picard divisibility

The projective module in Lemma <a href="#lem:horizontal-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:horizontal-expansion">26</a> must be locally trivial on the topology of $|D|$. The next elementary completion fact supplies this step.

<a id="stmt-lem-formal-line-extension"></a>
<div id="lem:formal-line-extension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *For the smooth affine chart and rings $A,B,R$ above, $B$ is a regular Noetherian ring. Every finite projective rank-one $R$-module extends to a finite projective rank-one $B$-module. Its associated line is locally trivial after restriction to formal Zariski neighborhoods of every point of $U\cap|D|$. The same assertions hold for $B^p\subset R^p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-formal-line-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-formal-line-extension</summary>

*Proof.* The Noetherian completion theorem gives that $B$ is Noetherian and flat over $A$, and $B/(f^n)=A/(f^n)$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemmas 10.97.1–10.97.5) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[stacks](#ref-stacks)]. The element $f$ belongs to the Jacobson radical of $B$: an element congruent to $1$ modulo $f$ has an inverse by the convergent geometric series. More generally, an element invertible modulo $f$ is invertible in $B$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 10.96.6) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[stacks](#ref-stacks)].

Every maximal ideal $\mathfrak n\subset B$ thus corresponds to a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m\subset A$ containing $f$, and $\mathfrak n=\mathfrak mB$. For each $s$, completion modulo $\mathfrak m^s$ does nothing, since $f^s$ already vanishes there. Hence $$B/\mathfrak n^s\simeq A/\mathfrak m^s,
 \qquad
 \widehat{B_{\mathfrak n}}\simeq\widehat{A_{\mathfrak m}}.$$ The latter ring is regular, as $A_{\mathfrak m}$ is a smooth local ring. A Noetherian local ring is regular if and only if its maximal-ideal completion is regular (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 15.44.4) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[stacks](#ref-stacks)], so every $B_{\mathfrak n}$ is regular. Localization now gives regularity at every prime of $B$.

A regular Noetherian ring is locally factorial (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 15.123.2) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[stacks](#ref-stacks)]. Its finitely many irreducible components are disjoint, since each local ring is a domain. On each component choose a rational section of the given line on $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R$ and take its Weil divisor. The closure of this finite Weil divisor on $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B$ is Cartier by local factoriality. Its line bundle restricts to the original one. Doing this on all components gives the asserted extension. This is the Cartier–Weil correspondence on a regular scheme (Hartshorne 1977, II, §6) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[hartshorne1977](#ref-hartshorne1977)]; global unique factorization of $B$ is not required.

Let $z\in U\cap|D|$. The extended line is free on a basic open $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}B_g$ containing the prime corresponding to $z$. Choose $g_0\in A$ whose image modulo $f$ equals that of $g$. Then $g_0(z)\ne0$. On the formal basic neighborhood coming from $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A_{g_0}$, its ring is $B'=\widehat{A_{g_0}}$, and the image of $g$ is invertible: modulo $f$ it equals the invertible element $g_0$. The line is therefore free on this formal neighborhood, and remains free after inverting $f$. Finally, Frobenius is an abstract ring isomorphism from $B$ onto $B^p$, and from $R$ onto $R^p$, because these rings are reduced. It identifies the corresponding formal basic neighborhoods. Thus the same proof applies to the power subrings. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-picard-divisibility"></a>
<div id="prop:picard-divisibility" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28** (Divisibility of the Laurent Picard group). *Multiplication by $p$ on $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(|D|,\mathcal R)$ is surjective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-picard-divisibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-picard-divisibility</summary>

*Proof.* Give an arbitrary $\mathcal R$-line bundle $\mathcal L$ the connection from Lemma <a href="#lem:cartier-fixed-connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cartier-fixed-connection">24</a>. On a chart with a line frame, Lemma <a href="#lem:cartier-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cartier-curvature">25</a> gives commuting nilpotent connection operators, and Lemma <a href="#lem:horizontal-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:horizontal-expansion">26</a> makes the horizontal module finite projective of rank one over $R^p$. Its restriction compatibility and intrinsic definition glue these modules into a sheaf $\mathcal H=\ker\nabla$ over $\mathcal R^p$. Lemma <a href="#lem:formal-line-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:formal-line-extension">27</a> shows that $\mathcal H$ is locally free of rank one in the required formal Zariski topology. The local multiplication isomorphisms glue to $$\mathcal R\otimes_{\mathcal R^p}\mathcal H\simeq\mathcal L.$$

Transport $\mathcal H$ through the sheaf isomorphism $\mathcal R\to\mathcal R^p$, $a\mapsto a^p$, to obtain a line bundle $\mathcal N$ over $\mathcal R$. Extension from $\mathcal R^p$ to $\mathcal R$ is its absolute Frobenius pullback. In local frames, that pullback raises the transition units of $\mathcal N$ to their $p$-th powers, which are also the transition units of $\mathcal N^{\otimes p}$. Thus $$\mathcal L\simeq\mathcal N^{\otimes p}.$$ This is a global root on the stated ringed space, proving the surjectivity assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Orders of units and an integral degree

Infinite Frobenius divisibility forces integral numerical invariants to vanish; this is also the numerical mechanism for stratified bundles in (Esnault and Mehta 2010, Lemma 2.1 and Corollary 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[esnaultmehta2010](#ref-esnaultmehta2010)]. Here the required degree is defined first on the completion. We must prove that it descends to the Laurent Picard group before applying that elementary divisibility obstruction.

<a id="stmt-lem-laurent-unit-orders"></a>
<div id="lem:laurent-unit-orders" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:laurent-unit-orders"></a>
**Lemma 29**. *Let $\iota_\alpha:D_\alpha%
  \mathrel{%
    \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=21AA}%
    \lhook\joinrel\rightarrow%
    \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
  }%
|D|$ be the inclusion of a prime component. There is an exact sequence of abelian sheaves $$1\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal O}^{\,\times}
 \longrightarrow\mathcal R^\times
 \xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{ord}\ }
 \bigoplus_\alpha(\iota_\alpha)_*\mathbb Z
 \longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:laurent-unit-orders}$$ Consequently restriction gives a surjection $$\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(|D|,\widehat{\mathcal O})\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(|D|,\mathcal R),$$ whose kernel is generated by the completed restrictions of $\mathcal O_V(D_\alpha)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-laurent-unit-orders"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-laurent-unit-orders</summary>

*Proof.* Work near a point on an affine chart as above, small enough that each component through it has a prime equation $f_\alpha$, and write $f$ as a unit times $\prod_\alpha f_\alpha^{m_\alpha}$. Every $f_\alpha$ remains a non-zero-divisor in $B$, by flatness of completion. It also remains prime, because <a id="eq:prime-completion-quotient"></a>$$B/(f_\alpha)\simeq A/(f_\alpha).
 \label{eq:prime-completion-quotient}$$ Indeed, the induced $f$-adic topology on $A/(f_\alpha)$ is the zero-ideal topology, so completion leaves this integral ring unchanged.

If $u\in B[1/f]^\times$, write $u=a/f^r$ and $u^{-1}=b/f^s$, with $a,b\in B$. Since $f$ is a non-zero-divisor, $ab=f^{r+s}$ in $B$. Successively use primality to cancel each of the finitely many prime factors of the right side from either $a$ or $b$. At the end the remaining factors multiply to a unit. This expresses $u$ uniquely as <a id="eq:laurent-unit-factorization"></a>$$u=v\prod_\alpha f_\alpha^{n_\alpha},
 \qquad v\in B^\times,\quad n_\alpha\in\mathbb Z.
 \label{eq:laurent-unit-factorization}$$ Uniqueness follows from the same prime cancellation. The exponents agree on smaller charts and are locally constant along the respective $D_\alpha$. They define the order map. Its kernel consists exactly of formal units, and the local equations give its local surjectivity, proving Equation <a href="#eq:laurent-unit-orders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laurent-unit-orders">[eq:laurent-unit-orders]</a>.

On an irreducible space with its Zariski topology, the constant sheaf $\mathbb Z$ is flasque: every nonempty open is irreducible, its locally constant integer-valued functions are constant, and all restriction maps are surjective. Each pushed-forward sheaf $(\iota_\alpha)_*\mathbb Z$, and their finite direct sum, is therefore flasque. Its first cohomology vanishes. Line bundles on a ringed space are classified by the first cohomology of its unit sheaf. The cohomology sequence of Equation <a href="#eq:laurent-unit-orders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laurent-unit-orders">[eq:laurent-unit-orders]</a> consequently proves surjectivity of the Picard restriction map. The image of the connecting map from the global integer sections is the subgroup generated by $\mathcal O_V(D_\alpha)$, as is seen from their local equations. Exactness proves the kernel assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-laurent-degree-contradiction"></a>
<div id="prop:laurent-degree-contradiction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *The prepared cycle of Proposition <a href="#prop:prepared-cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prepared-cycle">2</a> cannot satisfy the conclusions of Corollary <a href="#cor:form-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:form-vanishing">22</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-laurent-degree-contradiction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-laurent-degree-contradiction</summary>

<a id="eq:positive-laurent-degree"></a>
*Proof.* For a line bundle $\mathcal L$ on the completion, its restriction to each integral projective surface $D_\alpha$ is an ordinary line bundle. Define the integer <a id="eq:formal-integral-degree"></a>$$\Phi(\mathcal L)=
 \sum_\alpha m_\alpha
   \bigl(c_1(\mathcal L|_{D_\alpha})\cdot H|_{D_\alpha}\bigr).
 \label{eq:formal-integral-degree}$$ Intersection with the Cartier class $H$ gives an integral degree on each surface, so $\Phi$ is an additive homomorphism to $\mathbb Z$. The numerical properties of the prepared cycle give $$\Phi(\mathcal O_V(D_\beta))
    =\sum_\alpha m_\alpha D_\alpha D_\beta H
    =DD_\beta H=0.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:laurent-unit-orders" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:laurent-unit-orders">29</a> therefore makes $\Phi$ descend to a homomorphism $$\overline\Phi:\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(|D|,\mathcal R)\longrightarrow\mathbb Z.$$ It is nonzero, since $$\overline\Phi(\mathcal O_V(H)|_{\mathcal R})=DH^2>0.
 \label{eq:positive-laurent-degree}$$

On the other hand, Proposition <a href="#prop:picard-divisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:picard-divisibility">28</a> implies that every class in this Picard group is divisible by $p^n$ for every positive integer $n$. Its image under $\overline\Phi$ must therefore be an integer divisible by every $p^n$, hence zero. This contradicts Equation <a href="#eq:positive-laurent-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-laurent-degree">[eq:positive-laurent-degree]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-laurent-degree-contradiction-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-laurent-degree-contradiction</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The assumption of maximal nef dimension supplied the prepared cycle and all the conclusions of Corollary <a href="#cor:form-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:form-vanishing">22</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:laurent-degree-contradiction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:laurent-degree-contradiction">30</a> rules out that assumption. Thus $n(X,K_X)\le2$ over the uncountable algebraically closed extension used in the preparation. The nef-dimension-at-most-two abundance theorem (Z. Xu 2024, Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[xu2024](#ref-xu2024)] applies to the projective log canonical threefold pair $(X,0)$ in characteristic $p>3$ with nef canonical divisor. It makes $K_X$ semiample there.

To finish over the original field, denote it by $k_0$ and the extension by $k$. Restore $X$ for the original threefold and write $X_k=X\times_{k_0}k$. Choose a globally generated Cartier multiple of $K_{X_k}$, then take a further positive multiple divisible by the Cartier index of $K_X$ over $k_0$. Call the resulting integer $m$. The line bundle $\mathcal O_{X_k}(mK_{X_k})$ is still globally generated and is not numerically trivial, since $\nu(K_{X_k})=1$. It has at least two independent sections: if a globally generated line bundle has only one, that section trivializes it, contradicting its nonzero numerical class.

Compatibility of the canonical Cartier multiple with field extension and proper section-space base change give $$H^0\bigl(X,\mathcal O_X(mK_X)\bigr)\otimes_{k_0}k
   \simeq
 H^0\bigl(X_k,\mathcal O_{X_k}(mK_{X_k})\bigr).$$ Their dimensions are equal, proving the required inequality over $k_0$. In particular $\kappa(X,K_X)\ge1$; nefness and $\nu(K_X)=1$ give $\kappa(X,K_X)=1$, and the established positive-Kodaira-dimension abundance theorem (Z. Xu 2024, Theorem 1.6) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[xu2024](#ref-xu2024)] also gives semiampleness over the original field. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>

</div>

</div>

Cossart, Vincent, Uwe Jannsen, and Shuji Saito. 2009. “Canonical Embedded and Non-Embedded Resolution of Singularities for Excellent Two-Dimensional Schemes.” <https://arxiv.org/abs/0905.2191>.

</div>

<div id="ref-cossartpiltant2008" class="csl-entry">

Cossart, Vincent, and Olivier Piltant. 2008. “Resolution of Singularities of Threefolds in Positive Characteristic. I. Reduction to Local Uniformization on Artin–Schreier and Purely Inseparable Coverings.” *Journal of Algebra* 320 (3): 1051–82. <https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jalgebra.2008.03.032>.

      "explanation": "The abundance theorem for terminal threefolds serves as the indispensable foundation and base case before extending the result to singular pairs with boundary.",
      "relation": "prerequisite",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-035.2",
      "source_quote": "Then some positive Cartier multiple of $K_X$ has at least two independent global sections. In particular, $\\kappa(X,K_X)=1$ and $K_X$ is semiample.",

      "target_pm_id": "PM-035.1",
      "target_quote": "If $K_X+B$ is nef and has numerical dimension one, then $K_X+B$ is semiample.",
